The present: June to 20 November 2026

Open the note

US market drop, June – 20 Nov 2026: the present

Research notes compiled 20 Nov 2026 (evening, after the close). Raw material, not polished.

How to read this file

  • Everything about 2026 comes from pages fetched on 20 Nov 2026. Source keys like [S12] point to the source list at the end; the timeline table carries full URLs.
  • "Not found" means I could not establish it. The web-search allowance for this session ran out part-way through, so the later part of the work used direct page fetches only. Positioning data (section 5) and strategist views (section 7) are the thinnest as a result.
  • Items marked (memory) are 2018 or pre-2026 background from my own training knowledge, not re-fetched today. Check them against the history agents' files.
  • Items marked (my arithmetic) are computed from sourced numbers.
  • Many day-by-day details come from secondary write-ups (AP wire copies, CNBC live blogs, Substack recaps). Index closes were cross-checked across at least two of them where possible.

1. Timeline, June – 20 Nov 2026

Background needed to read the table (not part of the June–Nov window):

  • 28 Feb 2026: US and Israeli strikes on Iran begin the 2026 Iran war; Iran blocks the Strait of Hormuz. Brent goes from $72.48 (27 Feb) to $118.35 (31 Mar). [S15] [S16]
  • 30 Mar 2026: S&P 500 low of the war sell-off, 6,316.91; Nasdaq closed in correction in March. [S16] [S17]
  • 7–8 Apr 2026: ceasefire; 30 Apr Brent hits $126 intraday (four-year high). [S15]
  • 22 May 2026: Kevin Warsh sworn in as Fed chair (confirmed 54–45 on 13 May). [S18]
  • 2025 items (April 2025 tariff crash, autumn 2025 shutdown, Fed cuts Sep 2024 – Dec 2025) are background only and are not in the table.

Other dated items that did not fit one row:

  • Fed politics. Trump nominated Warsh on 30 Jan 2026 to deliver low rates; before the September vote he called the committee "clowns" for wanting to hike; after it he said rates should be "1%, or less". The administration restarted its effort to remove Governor Cook, with a hearing set for early November (outcome not found). Warsh said politics played "no role whatsoever". [S18] [S19] [S20] [S21]
  • Global tightening. The ECB raised rates in June and September 2026; Australia raised to 4.85% on 3 Nov; New Zealand is expected to hike on 9 Dec. [S16] [S22] [S3]
  • Oil in November. Week of 2–6 Nov WTI fell 13.2% ($104.12 to $90.37). On 18 Nov WTI hit $82.19 on rising US inventories and Hormuz shipments back near pre-war levels. On 19–20 Nov there were new tanker-mine incidents and Houthi missiles hit Riyadh; Trump said he was in no rush to decide on Iran. Rapidan's Bob McNally (6 Nov) put 80% odds on renewed US–Iran fighting. [S7] [S23] [S4] [S24] [S25]
  • Trade. No new tariff shock found in the window. Caterpillar cited about $2.2bn of tariff costs (29 Oct). RTX booked "IEEPA cost recovery" in Q3, which implies tariff refunds are flowing. Trump–Xi at APEC (18–19 Nov) changed nothing on chips or tariffs. [S8] [S26] [S5]
  • Shutdown. Yahoo (20 Nov) lists "a possible U.S. government shutdown in December" among investor worries. The deadline date was not found. [S27]
  • Outages. FAA network failure and ground stop (about 13 Nov), Microsoft outage (12 Nov), Zelle slowdown at JPMorgan and United systems outage (16 Nov). Cited as adding to unease. [S28] [S27]

Good news sold

19 NovAnthropic IPO priced above range, opened +34%Market peaked at the first trade; S&P swung from +1.3% to -2.0%[S3] [S36]

Bad news bought, or shrugged off

  • 9–13 Nov: after the -4.4% day the S&P gained 2.5% on the week; chips rose 5–9% in the week Nvidia lost its CEO (AMD and Broadcom +18%). [S37] [S38]
  • Energy stocks +5.4% in a week when oil fell (9–13 Nov). [S37]
  • FactSet, through 13 Nov: S&P 500 companies with negative EPS surprises fell only 1.1% on average (five-year average -3.1%); positive surprises gained 1.1% (average +0.9%). [S39]
  • The exception inside that: the three Magnificent 7 beats (Apple, Microsoft, Tesla) averaged -0.2%; the three misses (Alphabet, Amazon, Meta) averaged -7.8%. [S39]

Pattern commentators describe

  • "Sell the rally": CNBC's 19 Nov framing; Steve Sosnick (Interactive Brokers): "The dam seemed to break once the big event was behind us." [S3]
  • Until 18 Nov it was a rotation, not a liquidation: the equal-weight S&P beat the cap-weighted index five weeks running, and on 13 Nov equal-weight was +16.8% year to date against +11.5% for SPY. On 19–20 Nov it became broad: 464–37 on Thursday, decliners 12:1 (Wolfe: worst breadth since December 2025); Procyon: "becoming broader... definitely risk-off". [S37] [S4] [S1]
  • Motley Fool's 19 Nov wrap: inflation, rates and valuations are "taking precedence over strong corporate results". [S35]
  • Bank of America's Wamsi Mohan (6 Nov): the AI sell-off is multiples falling, not estimates falling. FactSet agrees: since 30 Sep the index price fell 6.2% while the forward EPS estimate rose 4.4%. [S25] [S40]
  • Three gap-up-and-fade sessions in the last six (13, 17, 19 Nov). [S34] [S28] [S3]
  • Bonds: until mid-November yields rose with oil and inflation and hurt stocks. On the three worst equity days (5, 19, 20 Nov) yields fell. Gold fell with stocks (Robin Brooks: "panic and liquidation"), and the dollar rose to a 19-month high. [S6] [S5]

Sector and megacap detail:

  • Energy EPS +151% y/y (average WTI $85.68 in Q3 vs $64.97 a year earlier; refiners +542%). Information technology +69% (semiconductors +133%; ex-semis +30%; software +17%). Financials +6.4%. Communication services cut to +18% from +52% expected because of Meta's $9.9bn charge and Alphabet's $8.7bn investment loss. [S39]
  • Magnificent 7 Q3 EPS growth 27.0% vs 34.7% (FactSet's text also says 32.6%) for the other 493: the first quarter the seven grew slower than the rest since Q4 2022. Expected again in Q4 (22.6% vs 30.8%). [S40]
  • Forward P/E by sector (13 Nov): consumer discretionary 25.1, staples 23.7, industrials 22.5; energy 12.2, financials 15.1. Tech P/E not captured. [S39]
  • The Fed's view (FSR, 13 Nov, data mostly through October): valuations "remained elevated"; forward P/E "well above its historical median"; the equity premium "near the lower end" of its historical range. With a 10-year real yield near 2.9% (TIPS auction 19 Nov at 2.903%), a 17 P/E (5.8% earnings yield) leaves a premium near 3 points (my arithmetic). [S42] [S5]

Versus Sep–Dec 2018 (memory, from FactSet reports of the time): Q3 2018 EPS growth about +26% (tax-cut year), CY2018 about +20%, CY2019 estimates were being cut from about +10% to +7–8% during the drop; forward P/E about 16.8 at the September 2018 high, about 13.9–14.2 at the December low. So in 2018 the multiple fell roughly 17% peak to trough with estimates slipping. In 2026 the multiple has fallen about 15% (19.4 to 16.5) in six weeks with estimates still rising.

4. Leading themes and how they are behaving

AI / semiconductors (led up, leading down)

  • Nvidia: record valuation about $5.7tn in early October; -9% on 5 Nov; Huang out 12 Nov; $145.98 on 19 Nov; -14% on 20 Nov; -23% on the week, worst week since October 2008; -28% in November before Friday. HSBC downgraded to hold (target $170 from $365); Daiwa cut to $185. [S32] [S2] [S3] [S34] [S4]
  • AMD: -17% (5 Nov), -7.7% (19 Nov, short report), -13% (20 Nov); week -20.5%, worst since October 2018. [S32] [S4] [S2]
  • Micron: $778 on 13 Nov; -14% on 20 Nov; about -22% in five sessions. TSMC -20% on the week (worst since July 2002). Broadcom $346.65 on 19 Nov, double-digit fall on 20 Nov. [S37] [S2] [S5]
  • Indexes: SOX -12% on 5 Nov; SMH 487 on 13 Nov against a 200-day of 524; SOXX on 20 Nov back to its 9 April level; all 20 chip stocks in the Nasdaq-100 in bear markets; 71 of 101 Nasdaq-100 stocks down 20% or more. VanEck said SMH was about 33% below its June high on 5 Nov (I could not reconcile this with Nvidia's early-October record). [S23] [S38] [S1] [S2] [S5]
  • Asia: Kospi -14.5% and SK hynix -24% in the week of 16 Oct; Taiwan -10% in the week to 19 Nov, with short-selling curbs from 20 Nov; Nikkei -5.1% on 20 Nov. [S30] [S4] [S2]
  • Storage/hardware: Seagate, Western Digital, SanDisk, Marvell -27% to -28% and Dell -23% in the week of 6 Nov. Optical: Applied Optoelectronics -31%, Coherent and Lumentum -25% in the week of 16 Oct. Intel -28.6% on 30 Oct. [S7] [S30] [S8]

The Fed compares the pace with the 1995–2001 telecom build-out and says AI capex is about to exceed it as a share of GDP. [S42]

Nebius plans $40–45bn of 2027 capex, over three times expected revenue. Nscale's IPO filing says it needs more financing for 2027. Lenders cut GPU loan-to-value from about 80% to 50–60%. Microsoft tightened oversight of Nebius, IREN and Nscale projects. SoftBank booked a $3.7bn loss on cancelled data-centre power equipment. On 20 Nov: Applied Digital -16%, Nebius -12.5%, IREN -12%, CoreWeave -9%. [S23] [S3] [S38] [S5] [S2]

  • Margin debt rose 23% in the three months April–June 2026 and peaked in June, four months before the index. It did not make a new high with the S&P in October.
  • November's figure (due mid/late December) will show the deleveraging; not available yet.
  • 2018 comparison (memory, FINRA series): May 2018 peak about $669bn; September 2018 about $648bn; October 2018 about $608bn; December 2018 about $554bn, a 14% fall in three months. Relative to total US market capitalisation both periods are roughly 2% (about $648bn on about $30tn in September 2018; about $1.45tn on roughly $75–80tn in October 2026). The 2026 market-cap figure is my rough estimate; no source was fetched for it.

Hedge funds

  • Fed FSR (13 Nov): hedge-fund leverage "reached its highest level" since data began in 2013 (Form PF, Q1 2026), "record or near-record leverage in equity, relative value, and macro strategies", concentrated in a few large funds. Hedge-fund assets $13.3tn. Most dealers said hedge-fund and ETF client leverage was above the ten-year midpoint (September SCOOS). [S42]
  • October FOMC minutes: staff called elevated valuations and hedge-fund leverage "the most salient concerns"; most participants named hedge-fund leverage or limited dealer capacity as amplifiers; several flagged banks' prime-brokerage exposures. [S13]
  • Morgan Stanley (cited 19 Nov): hedge funds are underweight large-cap tech (-7.2% active weight vs -6.3% in Q2) with tech at 40% of the index. [S5]
  • JPMorgan (cited 19 Nov): overall US equity positioning about the 20th percentile since 2015, but global semiconductor exposure about the 80th. [S5]
  • Nomura's Charlie McElligott (5 Nov): much of the selling was hedge funds locking in profits before year-end. Dan Ives (20 Nov) lists "forced selling" and fund losses reducing buying power among seven causes. [S32] [S2]
  • Michael Burry (16 Nov): keeps bearish positions on Nvidia, SMH and Palantir. [S24]
  • Goldman Sachs / Morgan Stanley prime-book gross and net leverage numbers for Oct–Nov: not found. OFR Hedge Fund Monitor figures: not retrieved. Named fund blow-ups or closures this autumn: none found.

Systematic strategies (CTA, vol-control, risk parity): no estimates found. Facts that bear on them: the S&P closed below its 200-day average on 18 Nov; 20-day realised volatility on 6 Nov was 20% for SPY, 33% for QQQ, 62% for SMH; the VIX curve inverted on 6 Nov (VIX/VIX3M 1.04), normalised by 13 Nov (0.94), and with VIX near 36 on 20 Nov is certainly inverted again. [S43] [S7] [S37]

Main similarities

  1. A Fed that is tightening and says so plainly, with a new-ish chair who does not want to give guidance; the president attacking the Fed in public.
  2. The sell-off started in early October from a record high and was led by the year's leaders (technology), with a midterm election in the middle and a one-day post-election rally that failed.
  3. Long yields at multi-year highs just before the top (3.24% then, 5.37% now), with the 10-year peaking the day after the index.
  4. Strong reported earnings and strong GDP (+3.4% both times) while prices fall: a de-rating, not an earnings collapse.
  5. A December FOMC with a hike expected, and shutdown risk in December.
  6. Margin debt already below its peak when the index peaked (May 2018; June 2026).

Main differences

  1. Inflation. 2018: about 2% and falling with oil; the Fed could stop (and did on 4 Jan 2019). 2026: 3.7% and rising, core PCE above 3%, PPI 6.5%, "above target for five and a half years". Almost all FOMC participants expect more hikes, two dissented in October for hiking faster, and a governor said on the day of a 3.7% fall that financial conditions are "too accommodative". A pivot is much harder. Verified from the minutes and Cook's speech. [S13] [S51]
  2. Where the Fed is in its cycle. 2018: hike nine, near neutral. 2026: hike one of a new cycle.
  3. Oil. 2018: collapsing (helped the pivot). 2026: war-driven and volatile; it has fallen about 20% in three weeks, which is the one development that points the 2018 way, but tanker attacks continue.
  4. The Fed's attitude to the stock fall. The minutes describe lower AI valuations as a possible "source of market discipline". Nothing comparable in 2018.
  5. The bubble element. 2018 had no single financed capex boom. 2026 has $680bn a year of AI debt, negative free cash flow at Alphabet, Amazon and Meta, record IPOs (SpaceX, Anthropic), and neocloud credit stress. That is the 2000 ingredient.
  6. Leverage. Hedge-fund leverage at a record in Fed data; margin debt 22% higher than a year ago. In 2018 margin debt was roughly flat on the year.
  7. Politics. 2018's election result was neutral for the leaders. 2026's result is read as directly negative for the leading theme.
  8. Speed. S&P -11.6% in 32 trading days so far, with -8.6% in the last week. In 2018 it took until late November to make a similar first low, then the December leg took it to -19.8%.
  9. Valuation starting point. Forward P/E 19.4 at the 2026 top vs about 16.8 in 2018; but estimates are rising now and were falling then.
  10. Safe havens. Gold and bitcoin are falling hard in 2026; bonds only started helping on the worst days.

The Fed

  • Warsh, 16 Sep: inflation "too high for too long"; focus is price stability. 28 Oct: conditions "tighter, but not tight"; AI investment is rate-sensitive; no guidance. [S19] [S21]
  • Waller, 10 Nov: 50 bp in December should be on the table. Logan: at least two more hikes, maybe faster. Goolsbee, 19 Nov: a larger December move is possible (headline only). Barr, 19 Nov: policy needs further adjustment. [S37] [S57] [S58]
  • Cook, 20 Nov (Santiago): inflation "unacceptably high"; conditions "too accommodative" because equity prices support spending; delay risks "larger rate increases" later. [S51] [S1]
  • FSR, 13 Nov: top cited risks are AI, a risk-asset correction, higher long rates, cyberattacks, fiscal sustainability. [S42]
  • No Fed official was found commenting on this week's fall itself.

2018 comparison: I could not find the X post or any named strategist making the autumn-2018 comparison (search allowance exhausted). The comparisons that are on record are to 2000 (Tudor Jones; the Fed's telecom-cycle box) and, for weekly statistics, to October 2008 and April 2025.

What they say would mark a low

  • Krinsky: durable lows come with fewer than 40%, often fewer than 20%, of S&P stocks above their 50-day average (now 50%), and after leaders such as Apple (3% off its high) have broken. [S1]
  • Motley Fool wrap: better breadth, stable Treasury yields, S&P back above its 200-day average (about 7,350). [S35]
  • CNBC's historical note: tech tends to find footing over three to six months after such breaks. [S25]
  • Bespoke (via Yahoo): weeks in which the S&P fell every day with at least four 1% drops occurred in 1962, 1974, 1978, after 9/11 and in October 2008. [S2]
  • SentimenTrader (via Coffee Grounds): after downside reversals from record highs the S&P was higher six months later 92% of the time; the newsletter notes this week's reversal was not from a record. [S57]

Wed 23 DecNovember PCE and Q3 GDP third estimate[S59]
December, date not foundPossible government shutdown deadline[S27]
Date not foundHuang and Commerce Secretary Lutnick before a Senate panel; SEC inquiry into Nvidia; Cook removal case; Anthropic $26bn private placement after the offering closed on 20 Nov[S36] [S1] [S21] [S58]
Not verifiedTreasury auctions (2-, 5-, 7-year normally in Thanksgiving week; 3-, 10-, 30-year normally the week of 7 Dec); ISM on 1 and 3 Dec; December earnings from Oracle, Broadcom, Micron; debt ceiling; tariff or Supreme Court deadlines

9. Other relevant findings

  • Sequence of tops. FINRA margin debt June; Dow August; S&P, Nasdaq and Nvidia 6 Oct; equal-weight S&P still outperforming to mid-November. Leadership narrowed before it broke: Alphabet and Meta fell first (communication services -14% year to date by 30 Oct), then chips.
  • Three failed bounces since the high, each to a lower high: 7,757 (21 Oct), 7,744 (28 Oct), 7,691 (4 Nov, my arithmetic), then 7,619 (11 Nov). The first low was 7,353.73 (5 Nov); it broke on 17 Nov.
  • Three "second day worse" events: 4→5 Nov, 18→19 Nov, 19→20 Nov. Each started with a relief open.
  • IPO supply. $230bn raised in US IPOs in 2026 to 19 Nov (+533% y/y) on 36% fewer deals: Anthropic $81.3bn plus a $26bn placement, SpaceX, SK hynix. All 12 of the largest US IPOs since 2008 traded below their first price within a year. The 5 June rout preceded SpaceX; the 19 Nov reversal began at Anthropic's first trade. [S3] [S36] [S17]
  • Nvidia specifics. $227bn buyback authorisation unused; Q4 guide assumes no China data-centre revenue; gross-margin guide 71–72% against 74.9% in Q3. [S50]
  • Fed operating changes under Warsh. From 2027 one of eight meetings has no rate vote; no personal dot from the chair; balance-sheet working group reports around Q1 2027. [S21] [S18]
  • Taiwan restricted short selling from 20 Nov after a 10% weekly fall. [S4]
  • Consumer. Gasoline $3.69 on 20 Nov (from $4.37 in September); retail sales slowed in October; Walmart, Target and TJX reports were fine. [S1] [S36]
  • Housing. Homebuilder sentiment lowest since June 2012 (19 Oct); 30-year mortgage 7.22% (16 Sep). [S31] [S20]

2018: both drops and the recovery

14200 words.

Open the note

2018 US market drops: research notes (raw material)

Compiled 20 Nov 2026. Scope: the Jan–Feb 2018 "Volmageddon" correction and, above all, the 20 Sep – 24 Dec 2018 decline and the Jan–Apr 2019 recovery.

How to read this file

  • Every fact carries a source key in square brackets; keys resolve to URLs in the source list at the end.
  • [UNVERIFIED] = written from memory or from a single weak source and not confirmed against a page in this session. Treat as a lead to check, not as a fact.
  • The web-search budget for this session ran out part-way through. After that only direct page fetches of known URLs were possible (Fed, FINRA, CBOE, FRED, FactSet PDFs, Value Line daily recaps, CNBC, etc.). Gaps that result are listed in section 12.
  • Price levels and returns are deliberately sparse (you compute those). Where an index level appears it is quoted from the cited article.
  • Data pulled straight from primary files: FINRA margin statistics (xlsx), CBOE put/call history (csv), FRED (VIX, VIX3M, Treasury yields, WTI, mortgage rate, core PCE index), FactSet Earnings Insight PDFs (text extracted locally), Fed minutes/statements/FSR, BLS releases.

---

Category codes: Fed / trade / earnings / macro / politics / positioning / technical. VL = Value Line "Stock Market Today" daily recap for that date (URL pattern in source list).

2. Was the reaction to news a reliable indicator?

Short answer from the record: yes, as a confirming tool, not as a timing tool for the exact low. The reaction to news turned from "good news sold" to "bad news bought" within about seven sessions of the low, and stayed that way.

Good news sold (Oct – 24 Dec 2018)
dategood newsreactionsource
Q3 season (Oct)77–78% of companies beat EPS; aggregate surprise +6.5–6.8%; growth 25.9%, highest since Q3 2010As of 26 Oct, companies that beat were down 1.5% on average over the 4-day window (5-yr avg +1.0%): worst reaction to beats since Q2 2011. FactSet said it was "likely not due to EPS guidance or analyst revisions": negative guidance share (63%) was below average. By 2 Nov: beats +0.2%, misses −3.8% (avg −2.5%). Final (23 Nov): misses −3.2%[FS-1029], [FS-110218], [FS-112318]
11 OctSoft CPI (+0.1%)Morning rally sold; closed on the lows[VL 10-11-2018]
16–18 OctStrong bank/health earnings, record job openings+2.2% day reversed within two sessions; rates/China "overshadowing good corporate profits"[VL 10-16-2018], [VL 10-18-2018]
2 NovPayrolls +250k vs 188kOpened up, sold; closed down[VL 11-2-2018]
20 NovTGT, ROST, TJX: higher sales and profitsSold hard[VL 11-20-2018]
28 NovPowell "just below neutral"+2.3% day, but more new lows than new highs on the day; gains gone within four sessions[VL 11-28-2018]
1–4 DecG20 tariff truce+1.1% gap on 3 Dec, −3.2% on 4 Dec[VL 12-3-2018], [FORTUNE-1204]
19 DecFed cut its 2019 dots from three hikes to two, softened the statementSold: worst Fed day since 2011. The market wanted flexibility on the balance sheet and got "automatic pilot"[BBG-1219], [FOMC-JAN-MIN]
23 DecTreasury says banks have ample liquiditySold: raised a question nobody had asked[PBS-1224]

Bloomberg ran a piece on 17 Dec titled "Stock Selloff Defies Everything the Bulls Hoped Would Stop It" [BBG-1217] (text not retrievable in this session; title cited as evidence of the mood).

Bad news bought (from 26 Dec 2018)
datebad newsreactionsource
2 JanChina PMI in contractionDow −400 at the open, closed up[VL 1-2-2019]
2–3 JanAAPL revenue warning (−$5–9bn vs guide); ISM biggest drop since 2008−2.5% on 3 Jan, but the low held ~4% above 24 Dec; fully recovered the next day[VL 1-3-2019], [VL 1-4-2019]
15 JanJPM misses; Brexit deal defeatedMarket up[VL 1-15-2019]
22 JanChina GDP slowest since 1990; IMF cutOne down day, no follow-through[CNBC-0123]
Jan–FebCY2019 EPS estimates still being cut (growth forecast 7.4% on 4 Jan → 2.7% by June)Market rose anyway; forward P/E re-rated from 14.1 to 16.2 by 1 Mar[FS-010419], [FS-030119], [FS-062819]
Q4 season (Jan–Feb)Only 69% beat (below Q3's 78%)Beats +2.1% (avg +1.0%); misses −0.8% (avg −2.6%): the exact mirror image of October[FS-030119]
30 JanAAPL reports the weak quarter it had warned aboutAAPL +6.8% ("not as bad as feared")[R-0130]
What practitioners said
  • Mike Wilson (Morgan Stanley), 19 Nov: "Buying the dip has not worked in 2018 for the first time since 2002"; the only years it failed were bear-market years. On 20 Dec: institutions had been "trying to buy the dip to make up their year and they lost it every turn"; but also "this is not the place to be selling" [CNBC-BTD], [CNBC-MS-1220].
  • SentimenTrader (7 Jan 2019): the Nov and Dec rally attempts "had no strong thrusts, and both failed"; the post-24 Dec rally had two. The difference between a rally that fails and one that holds was breadth of participation, not the headline [ST-0107].
  • Fed minutes (Jan 2019), reporting market contacts: December FOMC communications were read as "not fully appreciating the tightening of financial conditions"; the late-December selloff was "amplified by poor liquidity and thin trading conditions around year-end" [FOMC-JAN-MIN].
  • JPMorgan (Kolanovic, Mar 2019): "just short covering, buybacks, dealers' gamma hedging, and some limited re-leveraging drove the entire recovery"; investors' equity exposure was still at the low end of its historical range in March [INV-KOL-0319].
  • I did not find a dated practitioner quote using the exact phrase "the market stopped going down on bad news" for 3 Jan 2019. The behaviour is documented above; the quote is not. [UNVERIFIED]

Caveat for the study: the market also shrugged off bad news for one or two days at earlier failed lows (23 Oct intraday recovery after CAT/MMM; 6 Dec recovery from −785 after Huawei; 10 Dec hammer). One bad-news reversal day was not sufficient. What distinguished January was the combination: bad news held above the low + 90% up days + follow-through + a policy change.

3. Fundamentals then

CY 201820.0–20.3%highest since 2010; effective tax rate 19.0% vs 25.7%[FS-122118], [FS-030119]

How 2019 estimates were cut:

  • CY2019 EPS growth forecast: 10.3% (21 Sep) → 9.4% (2 Nov) → 7.9% (21 Dec) → 7.4% (4 Jan) → 2.7% (28 Jun 2019) → 1.5% (9 Aug 2019) [FS-092118], [FS-110218], [FS-122118], [FS-010419], [FS-062819], [FS-080919].
  • CY2019 bottom-up EPS in dollars: $178.05 → $173.94 during Q4 (−2.3%; 5-yr avg Q4 cut −1.8%, 10-yr −3.6%). Q4 2018 EPS: $42.56 → $40.93 (−3.8%), the largest quarterly cut since Q3 2017, but in line with long-run averages [FS-0104H].
  • Negative Q4 guidance: 72 negative vs 33 positive (21 Dec) [FS-122118]. By Q2 2019: 87 negative, second-highest since 2006 [FS-062819].
  • Reading: the estimate cuts in Q4 2018 were ordinary in size. Price fell 15% while forward EPS rose 0.3% [FS-122118]. The drop was a multiple event, not an earnings event. Earnings then did go negative in 2019, and the market rose through it.

Valuation (forward 12-month P/E, FactSet):

FactSet on 21 Dec: the last time the forward P/E was 14.2 or lower was 16 Oct 2014. Sector extremes on 21 Dec: financials 10.2, consumer discretionary 17.8. Bottom-up 12-month target 3,128 = +26.8% above the index (vs +8.8% at the September high) [FS-122118], [FS-092118].

Fed
  • Fed funds target: 1.25–1.50% at the start of 2018; four hikes (Mar, Jun, 26 Sep, 19 Dec) to 2.25–2.50% [FOMC-DEC-MIN], [VL 9-26-2018].
  • Dot plot, median hikes for 2019: three (Sep 2018) → two (Dec 2018) → zero (Mar 2019) [CNBC-DOTS-SEP], [YAHOO-1219], [YAHOO-0320].
  • Balance sheet: runoff caps stepped up $10bn a quarter from Oct 2017 and reached the full $50bn/month ($30bn Treasuries + $20bn MBS) in October 2018, the month the decline began [CNBC-QT], [FOMC-DEC-MIN]. 20 Mar 2019: Treasury cap to be halved to $15bn from May, runoff to end in September 2019 [FOMC-MAR-BS].
  • The pivot sequence: 3 Oct "long way from neutral" → 28 Nov "just below" → 19 Dec hike + "automatic pilot" → 21 Dec Williams "open to reassessing" → 4 Jan "patient" + balance-sheet flexibility → 30 Jan formal pause → 20 Mar zero dots + end of QT. Seven weeks from the hawkish peak (3 Oct) to the first softening; five sessions from the low to "patient".
  • Fed staff/participants "placed little weight on balance sheet reduction as a prime factor" in the selloff, while acknowledging that some investors believed it [FOMC-JAN-MIN]. Morgan Stanley's Wilson argued the opposite in October: central-bank liquidity was being drained faster than investors expected and the selloff would not stop until they paused [CNBC-MS-1029].
  • Futures at year-end priced no hike in 2019 and a cut in 2020 as more likely than a hike [CNBC-1231].

  • Broad dollar index: 113.50 (20 Sep) → 116.22 (24 Dec) → 113.55 (31 Jan) [FRED-WTI]. The dollar's rise hurt multinational estimates; FactSet (21 Dec) showed lower 2019 growth for companies with high foreign revenue [FS-122118].
  • 30-yr mortgage rate: 3.95% (Jan 2018) → 4.94% peak (8–15 Nov 2018) → 4.55% (27 Dec) → 4.06% (28 Mar 2019) [FRED-MTG].
Oil
  • WTI settled at a four-year high of $76.41 on 3 Oct 2018 (the same day as Powell's remark and the Dow's record) and $42.53 on 24 Dec, the lowest since June 2017: about −44% in 56 trading days [CNN-OIL], [CNBC-OIL-1224]. FRED spot series: high 76.40 on 3 Oct, low 44.48 on 27 Dec [FRED-WTI].
  • Bear market by 8 Nov (10th straight decline, longest streak since 1984) [CNN-OIL]. Causes cited: Iran-sanction waivers, record US output, demand fears.
  • 26 Dec: +9%, largest one-day gain in over two years [AP-1226]. Oil and stocks bottomed the same day.
Credit
  • High-yield spread: 5.12% on 21 Dec, widest since July 2016 [HEDGEYE]; Reuters gave the peak as about 5.36% over Treasuries on 3 Jan 2019, then 108bp tighter within a month [INV-JUNK]. [UNVERIFIED] The FRED ICE BofA HY OAS series (BAMLH0A0HYM2) shows a peak of about 5.4% on 3 Jan 2019 and about 3.2–3.3% in early October; FRED now serves only three years of this series, so I could not re-pull it.
  • So the high-yield peak came seven trading days after the equity low, on the Apple/ISM day. Credit confirmed late, it did not lead.
  • No US junk bond priced between 30 Nov and 10 Jan (Dealogic via CNN); December was the first month without a junk deal since 2008 (another count: $594m, lowest since Aug 2008) [CNN-JUNK], [INV-JUNK].
  • BIS: HY spreads "briefly exceeded their pre-crisis average"; leveraged-loan spreads roughly doubled; large HY fund outflows with a bigger price response than in past episodes [BIS-0319]. Fed minutes: December leveraged-loan issuance lowest since July 2016 [FOMC-JAN-MIN]. Fed Dec minutes: spread widening concentrated worry on "lower-rated investment-grade" (BBB) issuers [FOMC-DEC-MIN].
  • No default cycle, no fund gating, no bank stress followed.
Activity data
  • ISM manufacturing: 61.3 in Aug 2018 (cycle high) → 54.1 in Dec (first print; −5.2 pts in a month, biggest drop since Oct 2008; later revised to 54.3) → 56.6 Jan 2019 → 54.2 Feb → 49.1 Aug 2019 → 47.8 Sep 2019 [ISM-DEC], [ISM-FEB], [ISM-SEP]. The manufacturing slowdown was real and continued for nine months after the stock low.
  • Jobs: +250k (Oct), +155k (Nov), +312k (Dec); unemployment 3.7–3.9%; wages +3.2% y/y in Dec [BLS-0119], [VL 11-2-2018], [VL 12-7-2018].
  • Housing: existing-home sales fell through 2018, −6.4% m/m in Dec to 4.99m (lowest in three years); full year −3.1%. Homebuilder stocks were already −21% YTD by 24 Sep (worst since 2008) [PBS-HOMES], [BBG-BUILDERS].
  • China: Shanghai Composite bear market from June 2018, four-year low mid-October, ended 2018 −24.6%; Nov retail sales growth weakest since 2003; RRR cuts in Oct 2018 (100bp) and Jan 2019 (100bp in two steps) [CAIXIN-BEAR], [BBG-CHINA-1015], [CNBC-OUTFLOW], [UKGOV-CHINA].
  • Inflation: core PCE index implies about 2.0% y/y for Dec 2018 on current-vintage data, falling to about 1.6% by Mar 2019 (my arithmetic on [FRED-PCE] index levels; real-time prints were 1.9–2.0% in H2 2018 [UNVERIFIED]). Inflation at or below target is what allowed the pivot: the Fed minutes cite "muted inflation pressures" [FOMC-JAN-MIN].

4. Leverage and positioning

Source: [FINRA] (primary xlsx, parsed directly).

  • Peak May 2018, four months before the price high; already a lower high in September ($648bn) at the price record: a divergence.
  • October −$40.5bn, November −$15.1bn, December −$38.3bn: Q4 total −$93.8bn (−14.5%), the largest quarterly drop since Q4 2008; −$114.6bn (−17%) from the peak [FINRA], [WOLF-0119].
  • Margin debt did not rebound with prices: $574bn in March 2019, still 14% below the peak and below December 2017, while the S&P was within 3% of its high [FINRA], [WOLF-0419]. It did not exceed the May 2018 peak until 2020.
  • As % of GDP: about 3.2% at the peak (my arithmetic, nominal GDP ≈ $20.6tn [UNVERIFIED denominator]). Free credit balances rose in December (cash raised): cash accounts $146.5bn → $159.6bn, margin accounts $178.8bn → $190.7bn [FINRA].
Hedge funds
  • Fed FSR, Nov 2018: financial-sector leverage low overall, but "some indicators suggest that hedge fund leverage is at post-crisis highs"; a comprehensive measure (margin, repo, derivatives) rose "by about one-third over 2016 and 2017"; dealers reported easier terms. Median fund leverage did not rise: the increase was concentrated in a subset [FSR-NOV18], [BROOKINGS].
  • Fed FSR, May 2019: gross leverage "rose steadily during the first part of 2018"; leverage "appears to have declined over the past six months"; "some indicators, particularly for equity hedge funds, show leverage fell at the end of 2018 as equity market volatility rose"; margin debt fell alongside [FSR-MAY19].
  • Goldman Sachs (Ben Snider, Nov 2018): the Hedge Fund VIP basket of most popular longs lagged the S&P by 725bp since mid-June (−9% vs −2%); a "vicious downward cycle" between returns, leverage and popular stocks [BI-GS]. The 50 most popular stocks were 32% of hedge-fund long books [BBG-CROWD].
  • Goldman / Morgan Stanley prime-book net and gross leverage numbers for Oct–Dec 2018: not found in this session. [UNVERIFIED] Recollection: GS prime book net leverage fell to multi-year lows by late December and stayed low well into Q1 2019, which is consistent with JPMorgan's March 2019 remark that investor exposure was at the low end of its range [INV-KOL-0319].
  • Performance: HFRI Fund Weighted Composite −4.7% in 2018 (prelim −4.1%), worst year since 2011; Preqin all-strategies −3.42% (worst since 2008); 59% of funds negative, 39% lost more than 5% [CMC-HF], [AICIO-HF].
  • Closures and outflows: liquidations 746 vs launches 609 (Preqin); investors pulled $22.5bn in Q4 and $34bn in 2018 (HFR). Closed or returned capital in 2018: Tourbillon, SPO Partners, Highfields (October), Omega (Cooperman), Criterion, Jabre. Greenlight −34%, its worst year [AICIO-HF], [BBG-HF-OUT], [BBG-OMEGA].
  • Momentum unwind: October saw a large momentum-factor drawdown; quant long-short funds −1.7% early in October; multi-factor ETFs had their worst month since launch (−7%); MSCI noted crowding in momentum "persisted" after October, i.e. the unwind was not complete [AXIOMA], [HEDGEWEEK], [MSCI-OCT]. NVDA (−54% in Q4) and FAANG were the centre of it [CNBC-NVDA], [FORTUNE-FAANG].
Systematic strategies
  • 5 Feb 2018: Kolanovic estimated outflows from volatility targeting, risk parity, CTAs and short-vol at "roughly $100 billion" [CNBC-XIV].
  • 10–12 Oct 2018: Kolanovic: option gamma hedging was the biggest systematic seller on 10 Oct; CTA selling fast and "largely behind us"; volatility-targeting (insurers, risk parity) to continue "several more days"; ~70% done by 12 Oct; offsets from buybacks (accelerated-repurchase programmes are not subject to blackout) and valuation buyers. Barclays (Deshpande): vol-control funds to sell ~$130bn [UNVERIFIED figure, secondary source] [BBG-KOL-1012].
  • 4 Dec 2018: McElligott (Nomura): CTA model "again deleveraging massive notional"; ~$42bn added on 3 Dec then cut to a fifth; next trigger near S&P 2,711. Nomura's own Takada and other banks' models disagreed (CTAs were already near neutral). Treat single-desk CTA numbers with caution [AFR-QUANT], [BBG-NOMURA].
  • Dealer gamma: long gamma "evaporated" as the index fell, leaving dealers short gamma and amplifying intraday swings; competing estimates [RISK-GAMMA]. Record SPX option open interest above 21 million contracts in December 2018 [SPDJI-OI].
Short-vol blow-up (5 Feb 2018)
  • VIX 17.31 → 37.32 in one session (+115.6%, a record). XIV ($1.9bn in assets) fell ~85% after hours and 93% the next day; its prospectus allowed acceleration if the indicative value fell to 20% or less of the prior close; Credit Suisse terminated it (last trade 20 Feb). SVXY −83% [CNBC-XIV], [CS-XIV], [NASDAQ-XIV].
  • Mechanism: inverse/levered VIX products had to buy VIX futures into the close in proportion to the move, which pushed VIX futures up further.
Liquidity
  • Goldman (18 Dec 2018): S&P 500 futures depth −70% over a year to a decade low; single-stock liquidity −42%; cause "simple risk aversion" of professional liquidity providers, not HFT or passive [FORTUNE-LIQ].
  • JPMorgan (Mar 2019): e-mini depth in Q4 "less than one-third the average seen during other sell-offs of the past decade"; with a third of the liquidity, the same flow moves price three times as far, in both directions [INV-KOL-0319].
  • CME: e-mini volume +65% while displayed book depth −75% vs Sep 2018 [CME-LIQ] (figure from search summary; page timed out on fetch).
  • A $50–100m large-cap block could take "a few days" to work [FORTUNE-LIQ].
Flows, tax selling, buybacks
  • Lipper: record $46.2bn out of US equity mutual funds + ETFs in the week to 12 Dec (partly distorted by ex-dividend dates) [CNBC-OUTFLOW].
  • ICI: long-term mutual funds −$56.2bn in the week to 19 Dec, largest since the week of 15 Oct 2008; December −$183bn (~1% of assets); ICI attributes ~$40bn to normal seasonality and part to tax-loss harvesting [INVNEWS-ICI], [ICI].
  • Lipper, December: conventional equity + mixed funds −$115.4bn (largest since at least Jan 2008), bond funds −$62.4bn, money-market funds +$63.1bn; ETFs +$50.1bn (equity ETFs +$35.2bn). Much of the "outflow" was a switch from mutual funds into ETFs, which is what tax-loss harvesting looks like [LIPPER-DEC].
  • SentimenTrader: four-week outflows from both stock and bond funds above 0.2% of assets a week, a 15-year record for both [ST-0107].
  • Tax-loss selling: Colas (DataTrek): S&P total return was still about flat YTD until 13 Dec; once a down year became likely, selling of losers accelerated from 14 Dec into "illiquid year-end markets"; estimate ~$41bn of tax-loss selling vs ~$25bn of offsetting ETF purchases; the 11 worst 2018 performers then gained 9.5% in the first weeks of January vs 3.6% for the index [YAHOO-TAXLOSS].
  • Pension rebalancing: Wells Fargo ~$60bn of equities to buy into year-end ($35bn large cap, $21bn small cap), "the largest rebalancing amount we've seen"; FT reported officials citing ~$100bn; effect "exacerbated by the low market liquidity" [FORTUNE-PENSION], [ASIATIMES-1226].
  • Buybacks: record $223bn in Q4 2018 and $806bn in 2018 [SPDJI-BB]. The October break coincided with the pre-earnings blackout window; I did not retrieve a dated Goldman buyback-desk source [UNVERIFIED].
  • Small options traders bought nearly 3 million puts in the week before 24 Dec (second-most on record); "Dumb Money Confidence" tied its record low [ST-1224].

At the low (24 Dec)
  • VIX closing peak 36.07 [FRED-VIX]. Note: lower than February's 37.32 although the price damage was twice as large.
  • 14.45% of S&P 500 stocks above their 200-day average, lowest since early 2016 [TC-1224]. Percent above the 50-day: [UNVERIFIED] recollection of about 1%, a record low; not confirmed.
  • S&P 500: 242 new lows, no new highs; all 11 sectors down, defensives (utilities −4.3%) joined the selling [R-1224], [CNBC-1224].
  • Insider buying: buyers outnumbered sellers by the widest margin since Aug 2011; number of buyers doubled in two months (Washington Service, via Bloomberg 26 Dec) [BBG-INSIDER].
  • Half-session, holiday liquidity, one session after expiration, year-end rebalancing pending.
  • CNN Fear & Greed: "extreme fear"; a reading of 2 is widely quoted but I found no dated primary capture [UNVERIFIED] [ETFTRENDS-FG]. Investors Intelligence: not retrieved [UNVERIFIED].

Order of events, compressed: sentiment extremes (−8 sessions) → put/call, new-lows and volume extremes (−2 to −1) → price low on a half-day with VIX peak and insiders buying (0) → 90% up day (+1) → higher low on bad news with less fear, credit peak (+6) → second 90% up day + follow-through + Fed pivot (+7) → breadth thrust (+10) → credit reopening (+11) → trend repair (+15).

Source: [CNBC-1224]. Note that materials, financials, industrials and staples had never exceeded their January 2018 highs: the September S&P record was made by technology, discretionary and health care alone. That narrowness is the "rolling bear market" Wilson described from May 2018 [CNBC-MS-1029].

  • Q4 2018 total return: energy −23.8% (worst), utilities +1.4% (only positive sector), S&P −13.5% [CALLAN].
  • Semis: SOX −19% in Q4 (to 21 Dec); NVDA −54%, AMD −45%, MU −33% [CNBC-NVDA]. SOX −6.6% on 24 Oct alone [R-1024].
  • FAANG, 19 Nov: FB −38.8% (from late July), NFLX −36.1% (late June), AMZN −26.3% (early Sept), AAPL −20.3% (3 Oct), GOOGL −20% (late July) [FORTUNE-FAANG]. AAPL made its low on 3 Jan 2019, after the index.
  • Homebuilders: −21% YTD by 24 Sep, i.e. broken before the index peaked [BBG-BUILDERS]. [UNVERIFIED] They bottomed in late October and made a higher low in December.
  • Banks: KBW bank index in a bear market by December; never regained leadership in 2019 ("struggling with the 10-year yield", StockCharts Feb 2019) [SC-0222].
  • Crypto-related: Bitcoin broke its $6,000 floor in mid-November and fell toward $3,200 by mid-December [UNVERIFIED levels]; NVDA's guide-down was the equity-market expression [FORTUNE-NVDA].
  • China tech / EM: bear market from June; EEM "bottomed late last month [October]"; Turkey, Brazil, Indonesia bounced double digits in November; Morgan Stanley upgraded EM to overweight in late November [CNBC-EM]. EM held its October low in December while the S&P fell another 10%.
  • Cannabis: [UNVERIFIED] TLRY's blow-off peak 19 Sep 2018, CGC mid-October (Canadian legalisation 17 Oct): the speculative top coincided with the index top.
  • Biotech small caps: [UNVERIFIED] XBI fell about 30% Sep–Dec and bounced hardest in January.
What defied the drop, and when it cracked
  • Utilities: highest close since Dec 2017 on 4 Dec, a day the S&P fell 3.2% [CNBC-UTES]. Cracked only in the last week: −4.27% on 24 Dec, worst day since Aug 2011 [CNBC-1224].
  • Staples, REITs: outperformed; all 11 sectors were negative for December [CNBC-1224].
  • At year-end "defensive stocks top 2019 playbooks" (Reuters, 29 Dec); forward P/Es of staples and utilities were above technology's, utilities the most expensive vs tech in 2.5 years; Evercore ISI called utilities 23% overvalued [R-DEFENSIVE], [BBG-UTES]. Consensus hid in defensives at the low; cyclicals and tech then led.
  • Gold / gold miners: [UNVERIFIED] gold bottomed mid-August 2018 (~$1,175) and rose through Q4; GDX rose in Q4.
What led off the low and through 2019
  • Software / cloud: "leading the tech sector, and broader market" by 15 Jan; first group to all-time highs (22 Feb); "held relative strength through the Q4 selloff and broke out on both an absolute and relative basis" [CNBC-0115], [SC-0222].
  • Payments: PYPL "breaking through multimonth resistance in a weak market" (Oppenheimer's Wald, 15 Jan); V +7%, MA +6.7% on 26 Dec [CNBC-0115], [AP-1226].
  • Semis: worst group in Q4, then SOX at a record by 23 Apr 2019; AMD (+148–153%), LRCX (+117%), KLAC (+100%) were the three best S&P 500 stocks of 2019 [BBG-0423], [CNBC-TECH19], [BENZINGA-19].
  • S&P technology sector +48% in 2019, best since 2009; AAPL +84%, MSFT +56% [CNBC-TECH19].
  • Consumer discretionary "the strongest of the five aggressive sectors" by February [SC-0222]. Retailers led on 26 Dec and 7 Jan [AP-1226], [VL 1-7-2019].
  • Tax-loss victims bounced hardest in the first three weeks of January, then faded as leaders [YAHOO-TAXLOSS].
  • Housing: [UNVERIFIED] builders were among the best groups of 2019 once mortgage rates fell from 4.94% to ~4% [FRED-MTG for rates].
  • 2019 IPO class: [UNVERIFIED] ZM (18 Apr), PINS, BYND (2 May), CRWD (June) led in Q2; UBER/LYFT failed.
Themes
  • Before (2017 – Sep 2018): FAANG and "momentum"; semis on crypto-mining and data-centre demand; tax-cut earnings boom (EPS +25%); cloud/SaaS; cannabis; small-cap biotech; small caps as a "tariff-proof" trade (Russell peak 31 Aug).
  • After (2019): enterprise software/SaaS and security; payments; semis and semi equipment (5G, memory recovery); Apple/Microsoft mega-caps; solar; housing; recent IPOs; restaurants/athleisure growth (CMG, LULU). FAANG as a block did not lead: only FB beat the tech sector in 2019, the others trailed it by at least 18 points [CNBC-TECH19].

Pattern to test with your data: the stocks that became leaders split into two types. (a) Relative-strength leaders (CRM, PYPL, COUP, TWLO, OKTA, ZS, VEEV, V, MA): made higher lows in December while the index made a lower low, and were at new highs within three to eight weeks. (b) Deep-oversold rebounders (AMD, ROKU, LRCX, KLAC, MU, AAPL): made lower lows with or after the index, far below the 200-day, and only became leaders after an earnings gap weeks to months later. StockCharts' and Oppenheimer's contemporaneous comments support type (a) for software [SC-0222], [CNBC-0115].

8. Character of the price action

The down-legs
  • Sudden, and not on scheduled news. 10 Oct (−3.3%), 24 Oct (−3.1%), 4 Dec (−3.2%) each erased one to three weeks of gains in a day. 10 Oct had no trigger at all; Kolanovic attributed it to option gamma hedging [BBG-KOL-1012].
  • Three legs: 3–29 Oct (rates + earnings reactions), 8–23 Nov (oil, Apple, NVDA, FAANG), 4–24 Dec (trade, curve, Fed, shutdown, year-end). Each started from a lower high and went to a lower low.
  • The final leg was a persistence event, not a single crash: from 14 Dec the S&P fell on 7 of 8 sessions (Russell 13 of 14) [CNBC-1224]; SentimenTrader on 20 Dec: first time since 2008 that 3 of 4 sessions fell 1.5%+ to a 52-week low, otherwise mostly a 1930s pattern [ST-1220].
Rallies inside the decline, and how they failed
rallytriggerlengthhow it endedsource
12–16 Octbank earnings, strong data3 sessions18 Oct rates/China; broke the low on 23–24 Oct[VL 10-12-2018], [VL 10-16-2018], [VL 10-18-2018]
30 Oct – 7 Novoversold; FB; Trump–Xi call; midterms7 sessions, to 2,813FOMC with no dovish shift (8 Nov), oil, Apple suppliers (12 Nov, Dow −602)[VL 10-30-2018], [FORTUNE-1107], [VL 11-12-2018]
26 Nov – 3 DecPowell "just below", G20 truce6 sessionsOne day (4 Dec, −3.2%)[VL 11-28-2018], [FORTUNE-1204]
10–12 Decundercut of Oct low and reversal3 sessions14 Dec China data; cascade[ST-1211]

Common features: started by a headline, biggest gains in the first one or two days (often gaps), led by the most beaten-down groups, no 90% up day and no breadth thrust [ST-0107], on 28 Nov more new lows than new highs [VL 11-28-2018], each stopped at a lower high. Wilson called the early-November bounce a "dead cat bounce" [BI-MS].

Intraday behaviour
  • Selling was a day-session phenomenon. Bespoke: "all of the declines have come during regular trading hours"; buying every open and selling every close would have lost 20.4% in 2018, while the overnight (close-to-open) strategy kept rising until the intraday one "started to collapse" [BESPOKE-INTRADAY, via Yahoo/AOL summary; original page 404 in this session].
  • Gaps up were sold; morning strength faded. 11 Oct (morning rally sold), 29 Oct (strong open, ~900-pt Dow range, close on the lows), 2 Nov (+200 → −300), 7 Dec (opened higher, sold all day), 11 Dec (opening gain gone by midday), 19 Dec (+300 → −352), 20 Dec ("sold off dramatically in the afternoon") [VL recaps for each date]. Bespoke flagged a reversal from +1.5% intraday to −1.5% at the close as the first since Feb 2009 [BESPOKE-INTRADAY].
  • Intraday recoveries from big losses happened repeatedly and did not mark the low: 23 Oct (−549 → −126), 6 Dec (−785 → −79), 10 Dec. Only 27 Dec (−611 → +260), which came after a 90% up day, held.
  • Liquidity: futures depth a third of normal; the same flow moved price three times as far [INV-KOL-0319]. This is why both the last down-leg and the first up-leg were so steep.
What day and swing traders said worked

Evidence here is thin and I will not invent it. What is documented:

  • Buying dips failed all quarter (Morgan Stanley's statistic; Wilson's "lost it every turn") [CNBC-BTD], [CNBC-MS-1220].
  • Selling strength / fading gaps worked, by construction of the intraday record above.
  • The IBD method had traders out: the market status was "in correction" until the 4 Jan follow-through day [MONEYSHOW-FTD]. An October follow-through attempt [UNVERIFIED] failed within days, as did one in late November/early December; this is the IBD experience most often cited, but I could not retrieve the dated IBD pages.
  • Kerry Given (MoneyShow, 7 Jan 2019) after the follow-through day: still cautious, "this market is going to have to show me that it has gotten over its 'sky is falling' fears" [MONEYSHOW-FTD]. Disbelief was the norm.
  • I found no first-hand, dated day-trader account for this period in the session. [UNVERIFIED / GAP]
The rebound: speed and the retest debate
  • 26 Dec: +5.0%. 27 Dec: intraday −2.8% (a buyer who waited got a second chance within 24 hours, about 2% above the low), closed up. 28 Dec – 2 Jan: sideways. 3 Jan: −2.5% on Apple/ISM, closing about 4% above the 24 Dec close: this was the only "retest", a higher low. 4 Jan: +3.4% and the follow-through. After 4 Jan the index never traded near those levels again.
  • So a trader had seven sessions (26 Dec – 4 Jan) in a ~7% range above the low, then it was gone. Ten straight days without a meaningful pullback followed; the S&P was above its 50-day on 17 Jan [BNN-0117].
  • Piper Jaffray's Craig Johnson (23 Jan): a 13% gain in 17 sessions had 12 precedents in 50 years; eight were V-bottoms after major lows, four were double bottoms that were not major lows [CNBC-0123].
  • Who expected a retest: Mike Wilson (Morgan Stanley), mid-Jan: S&P likely "back to its December lows at around 2,350", buy cyclicals there [BBG-MS-RETEST]. Tom Lee (Fundstrat): history says it may revisit the low; would buy it [YAHOO-LEE]. Adam Robinson: fast declines "statistically ... always been followed by a retest"; 4/7 odds it was a bear-market rally [YAHOO-RETEST]. Wellington Shields (Q4 review): "not yet time to signal an all-clear"; several months of basing is normal [WS-0119]. A strategist on CNBC as late as 11 Feb: "stocks are heading back to December lows" [CNBC-0211]. Many technicians called a V "possible" but "not the most likely scenario" [INV-RETEST].
  • Who did not: Jonathan Golub (Credit Suisse), 31 Dec: "the bottom has been put in this market"; fundamentals did not justify the fall [CNBC-1231]. Nick Colas: after a +6% January the market has "never been down for the year"; any retest "should be bought" [YAHOO-RETEST]. Frank Gretz (Wellington Shields), 18 Jan: a test is less likely given the strength and the "two 95% up-volume days" [WS-0119]. Dudack: December prices likely "the worst of the decline" [WS-0119].
  • Why the retest camp had a case: both prior lows of 2018 were retested (Feb → late Mar/2 Apr; Oct → Nov, then broken in Dec) [YAHOO-RETEST]. Why it was wrong: this time the cause (Fed) changed within seven sessions and breadth thrust signals fired, neither of which happened in February or October.
  • JPMorgan, March 2019: investors did not chase; exposure still low [INV-KOL-0319]. Margin debt confirms [FINRA]. The rally was climbed on under-positioning.

---

9. The January – February 2018 correction

  • Set-up: S&P record 26 Jan after one of the strongest Januaries on record; VIX 11.08 [FRED-VIX]; margin debt jumped to $665.7bn in January, +$23bn in a month [FINRA]; forward P/E 18.4 at the start of the year [CNBC-1231]; record-low realised volatility in 2017 and a crowded short-volatility trade (XIV $1.9bn) [CNBC-XIV].
  • Cause: 2 Feb jobs report with wages +2.9% y/y [BLS-0218] → fear of faster Fed tightening, yields up → Dow −666. Then a mechanical accident on 5 Feb: VIX +115.6% [FRED-VIX], inverse-VIX products forced to buy VIX futures, volatility-targeting and risk-parity funds forced to cut equities (Kolanovic: ~$100bn) [CNBC-XIV].
  • Speed: 10% in nine sessions (26 Jan – 8 Feb), then the fastest 10% correction from a record high on record [UNVERIFIED superlative] [PBS-0208].
  • Low: intraday washout and reversal on Friday 9 Feb ("bottomed out in early afternoon", bargain hunters before 2 pm) [VL 2-9-2018]. [UNVERIFIED] The low tagged the 200-day average.
  • Sentiment at the low was much milder than in December: total put/call 1.31 (9 Feb) vs 1.82 (20 Dec); equity put/call 0.88 vs 1.13 [CBOE-T], [CBOE-E].
  • Retests: a rebound to mid-March, then a second decline on tariffs (22–23 Mar) and a retest on 2 Apr (China retaliation, Amazon). VIX 23.62 on 2 Apr vs 37.32 on 5 Feb; total put/call peaked at 1.54 on 23 Mar [FRED-VIX], [CBOE-T], [VL 4-2-2018]. The 9 Feb intraday low held. A textbook retest with lower volatility, seven weeks after the low.
  • Time to new highs: Nasdaq and Russell 2000 by June [UNVERIFIED]; S&P 500 on 24 Aug, seven months after the peak [FORTUNE-0824]; Dow in late September.
  • What led: technology/FAANG, small caps and growth; industrials, materials, financials and staples never regained their January highs [CNBC-1224].
  • Fundamentals: earnings +24.6% (Q1) and rising estimates [FS-060118]; no credit stress. A positioning accident inside an earnings boom.
  • Difference from Q4: February was a volatility shock with fundamentals improving, so the low was a single washout plus a retest. Q4 was a growth/policy scare with estimates falling, so it took three legs and a policy reversal.

---

10. Comparison hooks: why late 2018 was a 20% correction, not 2000 or 2008

factorlate 2018in the words of
EarningsStill growing: +25.9% (Q3), +13.1% (Q4), 2019 forecast +7–8% at the low. The later "earnings recession" was two quarters of −0.3% / −0.4%FactSet [FS-112318], [FS-030119], [FS-010419]
Valuation resetForward P/E 16.9 → 14.2 in three months, below its 10-yr average; price −15% in Q4 while forward EPS +0.3%FactSet [FS-122118]. Golub: market "inexpensive", "fundamentals did not justify the pullback" [CNBC-1231]. Wellington Shields/UBS: fifth-largest one-year P/E contraction since WWII; median return after 26 such years +16% [WS-0119]
CreditSpreads peaked near 5.4% and reversed within days; issuance shut for six weeks, then reopened; no defaults, no fund failures of systemic sizeBIS: HY spreads only "briefly exceeded their pre-crisis average" [BIS-0319]; Fed minutes [FOMC-JAN-MIN]. For scale [UNVERIFIED]: ~8.9% in Feb 2016, ~20% in 2008
Banks / financial plumbingLeverage in the financial sector "low relative to historical standards"; hedge funds "do not play the same central role in the financial system as banks"Fed FSR Nov 2018 [FSR-NOV18]. Treasury: banks have "ample liquidity" [R-MNUCHIN]
Policy roomInflation at or below 2%; Fed able to stop: "patient" five sessions after the low, formal pause on 30 Jan, end of QT announced 20 Mar. JPMorgan: "an enormous shift in monetary policy"Fed [FOMC-JAN-MIN], [FOMC-MAR-BS]; [INV-KOL-0319]
Real economyPayrolls +312k in December, unemployment 3.9%, strong holiday sales; only manufacturing and housing slowingBLS [BLS-0119]; [AP-1226]
Nature of the sellingLiquidity and positioning: "poor liquidity and thin trading conditions around year-end"; depth a third of normal; tax-loss and systematic sellingFed minutes [FOMC-JAN-MIN]; Kolanovic: liquidity "explains how the market could go down and up so much on a relatively small change in positioning" [INV-KOL-0319]
Leverage already cutMargin debt −17% from its peak; hedge-fund leverage reduced; record fund outflows[FINRA], [FSR-MAY19], [LIPPER-DEC]
Framing by the bears themselves"Cyclical bear market within a secular bull"; "rolling bear market": sectors corrected one after another from January 2018, FAANG last. Wilson expected the global economy to bottom by Q2 2019 and said December was "not the place to be selling"Wilson [CNBC-MS-1029], [CNBC-MS-1220]

What it shared with real bear markets (so it could not be dismissed at the time): the first curve inversions since 2007, an ISM collapse, falling estimates, buy-the-dip failing for the first time since 2002, 2008-class breadth readings (new lows), a Nasdaq and Russell bear market, and the Fed still tightening into it. The Fed's own minutes show a few officials already opposed the December hike [FOMC-DEC-MIN]. The episode became "only" a correction because policy changed.

---

11. Anything else relevant

  1. The S&P's record was narrow. Materials, financials, industrials and staples topped on 26 Jan 2018 and never confirmed the September high; Russell and Nasdaq topped three weeks earlier; margin debt topped in May; homebuilders were in a bear market by September; EM and China since June. The index top was the last domino, not the first [CNBC-1224], [FINRA], [BBG-BUILDERS].
  2. Sentiment extremes arrived early and were useless for timing for two weeks. AAII bears at a five-year high, record outflows and the first undercut of the October low all occurred around 10–12 Dec with the S&P near 2,650; it then fell another 11% [AAII-1213], [CNBC-OUTFLOW].
  3. The capitulation readings clustered one to two sessions before the price low (put/call 20–21 Dec, volume 21 Dec), and the low itself came on a low-volume half-day. A trader waiting for a high-volume reversal day on the low never got one [CBOE-T], [R-1221].
  4. VIX made its high at the low (36.07), unlike textbook divergences; the divergence came on the 3 Jan higher low (25.45) [FRED-VIX].
  5. The earnings-reaction flip is measurable. Q3 season: beats −1.5% at the worst / misses −3.8%. Q4 season: beats +2.1% / misses −0.8% [FS-1029], [FS-110218], [FS-030119]. This can be tracked live every season from FactSet's weekly report.
  6. Price bottomed two quarters before earnings did, and while estimates were still being cut. Forward P/E 14.1 → 16.6 between January and June 2019 while 2019 growth forecasts went from 7.4% to 2.7% [FS-010419], [FS-062819].
  7. Leverage did not come back. Margin debt and hedge-fund exposure stayed low for months; the advance was not chased [FINRA], [INV-KOL-0319].
  8. Consensus at the low was defensive (utilities/staples as top 2019 picks, at P/Es above tech's) [R-DEFENSIVE]. The first week of January saw utilities lag and retail, energy, tech lead [VL 1-7-2019].
  9. Mutual-fund outflows overstated the selling: ETFs took in $50bn in December [LIPPER-DEC].
  10. Calendar mechanics mattered: full-size QT began in October; the buyback blackout fell in early October and again from mid-December [UNVERIFIED dates]; tax-loss selling from 14 Dec; quad witching 21 Dec; holiday half-day 24 Dec; pension rebalancing 26–31 Dec; new-year inflows.
  11. Official reassurance was read as alarm (Mnuchin, 23 Dec). The market turned when the cause was addressed (Fed, 4 Jan), not when officials said things were fine.
  12. Year-end funding stress showed up in repo (GC +260bp on 31 Dec), a preview of September 2019 [BIS-0319].
  13. 2018's three lows compared (Feb, Oct, Dec): Feb retested and held; Oct retested (Nov) and broke (Dec); Dec did not retest at all. The lazy rule "wait for the retest" was right once, lethal once, and cost the whole move once.

---

The 2000–02 and 2007–09 bears, and the AI comparison

49200 words.

Open the note

The two big bears, 2000–2002 and 2007–2009, and the AI boom against the dot-com bubble

Research file for the correction-versus-bear study. Compiled 20 Nov 2026, after the US close. Raw material: tables and dated facts, not prose.

0. Read this first

Three corrections to the brief
  1. The S&P 500 is no longer about 8% off its high. It closed at 7,175.83 on 19 Nov (−8.2%) and at 6,910.39 on 20 Nov (−3.70% on the day), which is −11.6% from the 7,818.93 record close of 6 Oct 2026, 33 sessions after the peak. The week was −8.6%. Sources: [YF1] [AP1] in section 9, and the caller's own yahoo__GSPC.csv, which gives the same figures [CALC].
  2. The Fed is hiking, not easing. It raised the funds rate on 16 Sep 2026 to 3.75–4.00%, held on 28 Oct with two dissents for a hike, and futures price a December hike at about 97% [FED1] [FED2] [YF1]. The 10-year yield reached 5.35% on 7 Oct [CN1]. This matters for every "the Fed will step in" comparison below: on policy direction, late 2026 resembles 2000 and 2022, not 2007 or 1998.
  3. Margin-debt levels. The often-quoted $278.5bn (Mar 2000) and $381.4bn (Jul 2007) are the old NYSE-only series, which could not be retrieved. FINRA's combined series, used here, gives $299.9bn and $416.4bn for the same months [FINRA].
How this file was produced, and its limits
  • The work was split into four research blocks run in parallel (2000–03 events; 2007–09 events and leverage; AI versus dot-com; final lows and leadership) plus the sections written directly here (3, 4, 5, 10, 11). I read all four blocks and assembled them; I did not re-open every source behind every cell.
  • Web search ran out early. The session's shared search budget (200 calls) was used up within the first minutes, so almost everything comes from pages fetched directly by URL. No search engine, proxy or archive was used as a workaround.
  • Blocked sources: money.cnn.com (HTTP 503 throughout), nytimes.com, washingtonpost.com, investors.com (IBD), goldmansachs.com (403), spglobal.com data files (403), web.archive.org. Consequences: few verbatim "buy the dip" press quotes, no Wall Street strategist year-end targets, no IBD follow-through-day designations, no Goldman bear-market PDFs, no S&P operating-EPS file, no long history of the ICE BofA high-yield spread (FRED now serves only three years of it).
  • "unverified" next to an item means no source for it was retrieved in this session; it is a lead, not a fact. The consolidated list is near the end.
  • (calc) / [CALC] / [Y] / "derived" mean arithmetic done here from retrieved daily data: Yahoo Finance daily closes, FRED series and CBOE VIX. For sections 2, 3, 5 and 10 I used the files already cached in scripts/marketdrops/.data/raw (read only). Your own computations may differ by rounding.
  • Press and private-research wording is paraphrased. Quotation marks are kept only for official documents (Fed, FOMC, White House, FCIC) and for titles.
  • Source keys in square brackets resolve in the source list at the end. The list is kept in five blocks, one per research block, because keys were assigned per block.

The findings that matter most
  1. Depth after six weeks told nothing. Thirty sessions after the peak the S&P 500 was −6.8% (Mar 2000), −9.6% (Sep 2000), −8.0% (2007), −6.7% (1973) and −6.8% (2022) in the bears, against −13.4% (1998), −6.7% (2011), −2.9% (2015) and −6.5% (2018) in corrections [CALC]. The two great bears began slowly; the fast early drops were mostly corrections.
  2. In both bears the consensus, the Fed's own staff and earnings estimates said "no recession" at this point. Fed report of July 2000: earnings forecasts still being revised up. Fed staff on 11 Dec 2007: the economy "avoids recession" (NBER later dated the peak to that month).
  3. What was different was under the index. 2000: the leading index was already in a bear market (Nasdaq −37% by 23 May) while the Dow rose, the curve was inverted, the Fed was still hiking, IPO and margin channels were closing. 2007: financials, homebuilders, small caps and transports had peaked months earlier, credit never healed after August, banks were tightening, the Fed was already cutting.
  4. Fed cuts did not stop either bear. 2001: 475bp of cuts, three rallies of about 20% (S&P) and 40% (Nasdaq), each failing at a lower high, because the problem was profits and overbuilt capital at 25x forward earnings. 2007–08: 325bp of cuts in 7.5 months did not reach borrowers (mortgage and Baa yields rose) because the problem was solvency; the lows came on capital, asset-purchase and accounting news.
  5. Bear-market rallies are large and frequent. Nasdaq 2000–02: eleven rallies of 10% or more, three of them above 35%. S&P 2007–09: four of 10% or more, ten of 7% or more, the last +24% (20 Nov 2008 to 6 Jan 2009). Ten of the Nasdaq's twenty largest daily gains since 1971 fell inside the 2000–02 bear [CALC].
  6. Earnings. As-reported trailing EPS fell 54% in 2000–01 ($53.70 to $24.69) and 92% in 2007–09 ($84.92 to $6.86) [SHILLER]. In 2000 EPS peaked six months after the price peak; in 2007 four months before it. Forward P/E at the peaks: 25.2x (2000) but only 15.1x (2007): valuation flagged the first and not the second.
  7. Final lows: fear, volume and (in 2008–09) credit peaked months before price. VIX closed at 45.08 on 5 Aug 2002 against 42.13 at the October low; 80.86 on 20 Nov 2008 against 49.68 on 9 Mar 2009. The Baa spread peaked 4 Dec 2008. Growth indices held their earlier lows (Nasdaq-100 on 9 Mar 2009; Nasdaq and SOX in Mar 2003).
  8. Leaders of the next bull mostly bottomed long before the index and stood 27–115% above their lows on 9 Mar 2009 (AMZN, PCLN, BIDU, NFLX, CMG, CRM, MELI, FCX, GS). A second group (AAPL in 2003; LULU, ULTA, banks in 2009) bottomed with or after the index and gave no early clue.
  9. AI versus dot-com. On forward earnings 2026 is far from 2000 (S&P 18.3x on 12 Nov, about 16.7x at the 20 Nov close, against 25.2x; the tech sector is below its 25-year average). On the capital cycle it is beyond 2000: big-four capex was 115% of operating cash flow in Q3 2026, combined free cash flow turned negative, $284bn of bonds were sold in 2026 to date, equity is being issued, and Oracle and CoreWeave credit is being repriced. Cisco, Intel and Lucent were profitable leaders and their earnings still collapsed within a year.
  10. Where Nov 2026 sits on the checklist (section 10): bear-side readings on Fed direction, leadership divergence (Russell 2000 and Transports below their highs at the record), margin-debt peak before the index peak, and equity supply; correction-side readings on index-level credit spreads (Baa spread 1.52, high-yield 3.52%), jobless claims (about 200k), bank lending standards (1.7) and earnings estimates (still rising, which was also true in spring 2000).

1. Timeline tables

Format: date | event | category | how the market took it | source. "How the market took it" is that day's (or week's) index move. Sub-sections and tables keep the labels of their research block (A = 2000–03, B = 2007–09 and leverage, C = AI and dot-com, D = lows and leaders) so that cross-references inside them still resolve.

1a. 2000–2003 (about 95 rows)

WP = the Wikipedia page named. MPR = Fed Monetary Policy Report. Percentages marked (calc) are computed from the closes on those pages. Shape of the decline (S&P closes, calc from [YH-SPX]): peak 9 Oct 2007 → −10.1% (26 Nov) → rally +7.7% → −18.6% (10 Mar 2008) → rally +12.0% (19 May) → −22.4% (15 Jul) → rally +7.4% (11 Aug) → −45.8% (27 Oct) → rally +18.5% (4 Nov) → −51.9% (20 Nov) → rally +24.2% (6 Jan 2009) → −56.8% (9 Mar 2009). BIS splits it into five stages: (1) to mid-Mar 2008 funding/liquidity, (2) to 15 Sep 2008 solvency worries, (3) 15 Sep–late Oct global loss of confidence, (4) late Oct–mid-Mar 2009 recession fear, (5) from mid-Mar 2009 stabilisation [BIS2].

Reading:

  • The trader's reference point. Session 30 after the 9 Oct 2007 peak was 20 Nov 2007 at −8.0%: almost exactly "8% off after six weeks". Session 33 was 26 Nov 2007 at −10.1%. In 2026 session 30 was −6.1% and session 33 is −11.6%.
  • What followed in 2007: a 7.7% rally in two weeks on Fed speeches, −6.2% at year-end, then a break of the November low on 15 Jan 2008 (section 2b).
  • What followed in 2000: from −11.2% three weeks after the March peak the S&P recovered to −0.4% on 1 Sep 2000. The decline that never recovered began from that second peak (section 2a).
  • Corrections were often deeper at this stage than the bears: −13.4% (1998) and, at session 63, −15.8% (2018). Price depth at six weeks does not separate the two cases.

Reading: a first close below the 200-day average came within 6 to 65 sessions in every case, correction or bear, so it separates nothing. The 200-day average itself turned down in both major bears (13 Oct 2000, 23 Jan 2008), but also in the 2011, 2015 and 2018 corrections; only 1998 escaped. The S&P was about 10% and 14.5% below its peak when the slope turned in 2000 and 2008, with further falls of about 43% and 49% still to come.

Reading: at both major-bear peaks other indices were already well below their own highs (Transports −29% in Mar 2000; Nasdaq −16% and Russell 2000 −11% at the Sep 2000 retest; Transports −8% in Oct 2007; Russell 2000 −7% in Jan 2022). In the 2011 and 2018 corrections everything peaked together. But 1998 and 2015 also showed divergence, so it is a necessary condition in this sample, not a sufficient one. At the 6 Oct 2026 record the Russell 2000 was 7.8% and the Transports 17.7% below their own highs.

2a. 2000: the first months as understood at the time
A2.1 Depth at the "about six weeks" and "about three months" marks

All closes from Star-Bulletin pages; percentages are (calc).

Nasdaq from 10 Mar 2000 (5,048.62)30 Mar: 4,457.89, −11.7%20 Apr: 3,643.88, −27.8%23 May: 3,164.55, −37.3%9 Jun: 3,874.84, −23.2%17 Jul: 4,274.67, −15.3%
S&P 500 from 1 Sep 2000 (1,520.77)22 Sep: 1,448.72, −4.7%13 Oct: 1,374.17, −9.6% (12 Oct: 1,329.78, −12.6%)20 Dec: 1,264.74, −16.8%1 Dec: 1,315.23, −13.5%12 Mar 2001: 1,180.16, −22.4%
Dow, for contrast10 Mar 2000: 9,928.82 (−15.3% from Jan peak, on the Nasdaq's record day)24 Mar: 11,112.7214 Apr: 10,307.325 May: 10,577.861 Sep: 11,238.78

Direct answer to the trader's comparison. Six weeks after its March 2000 peak the S&P 500 was 6.2% down, having been 11.1% down at three weeks. Six weeks after the 1 Sep 2000 retest it was 9.6% down (12.6% the day before). On the index alone, spring 2000 was indistinguishable from an ordinary correction, and it was fully repaired by September. The second leg, autumn 2000, is the one that never came back.

Official voices (verbatim, public documents):

  • FOMC, 16 May 2000: "Increases in demand have remained in excess of even the rapid pace of productivity-driven gains in potential supply."
  • FOMC minutes, 16 May 2000: "The greater risk to the economic expansion at this point was for policy to be too sluggish in adjusting." The same minutes noted the April fall in "speculative technology shares" and thought it might make consumers "somewhat less exuberant".
  • Fed Monetary Policy Report, 20 July 2000: corporate earnings "exceeded expectations, for the most part" and "projections of future earnings continue to be revised higher."
  • Greenspan, 13 Feb 2001: analysts' three-to-five-year earnings projections "have generally held firm at a very high level."

Not found (unverified): Wall Street strategists' year-end 2000 and 2001 S&P targets; any CNNfn or NYT "healthy correction / buy the dip" wording from April–May 2000 beyond the AP reports above.

A2.4 What the Fed was doing
DateActionStated risk
2 Feb 2000+25bp to 5.75%inflation
21 Mar 2000+25bp to 6.00%inflation
16 May 2000+50bp to 6.50%inflation
28 Jun, 22 Aug, 3 Oct, 15 Nov 2000holdstill inflation (some members wavering by Oct–Nov)
19 Dec 2000holdflipped to economic weakness
3 Jan 2001−50bp, inter-meetingweakness

So the Fed tightened for nine weeks after the Nasdaq peak, including its largest hike in five years one week before the May low, and kept a tightening bias for almost nine months after the S&P peak (to 19 Dec 2000). The 28 Jun hold is inferred from the Fed's rate table and the 22 Aug AP report.

A2.5 What could have distinguished it in real time

Leadership collapsing while the old economy rose3 Apr: Nasdaq −349, Dow +300. 15–16 Mar: Dow +819 in two days as Nasdaq corrected. Nasdaq −37% in ten weeks while S&P never closed more than 11% downSB pagesRead at the time as healthy rotation, not as a top
Narrow, deteriorating breadth before the peakMore stocks fell than rose in 1999. On 10 Mar 2000 the Dow was 15% under its high on the Nasdaq's record dayWP Dot-com bubble; SB 2000-03-10Divergence had persisted for a year
Fed tightening into the fallHikes on 21 Mar and 16 May (50bp); inflation bias to 15 NovFed sources aboveMarket rallied on each hike day
Yield curve10y–2y inverted from 2 Feb 2000 to 28 Dec 2000 (low −0.52 on 7 Apr). 10y–3m inverted briefly in April, then continuously from July 2000 to Jan 2001 (low −0.95 on 2 Jan 2001)FRED T10Y2Y, T10Y3MThe Fed's own July 2000 report blamed shrinking Treasury supply; swap and corporate curves were flat to upward
IPO windowIPO proceeds in US/Japan/Germany/UK fell from $49bn in Q1 2000 to $29bn in Q2; US deals cancelled or postponedBIS 71st AR; Fed MPR July 2000Brief revival in the summer rally
Margin debtSurged from late Q3 1999 to March 2000, then fell; down about 30% by year endFed MPR July 2000; MPR Feb 2001Dollar levels unverified
Volatility regimeNasdaq moved 5% or more on 27 days in 2000, versus 7 days in all of 1990–99Fed MPR Feb 2001Known only in aggregate afterwards; daily swings were obvious
Smart-money casualtiesTiger closed (short the bubble) on 30 Mar; Quantum managers out (long the bubble) on 28 AprWP pagesAnecdote
Lock-up supplyAcademic work links the internet-stock collapse to a wave of sellers as IPO lock-ups expiredOfek and Richardson, NBER w8630Published Dec 2001
Credit (autumn only)High-yield spreads "soared beginning in September"; default rate highest since 1991; bank tightening at its highest since 1990Fed MPR Feb 2001; FOMC minutes Oct–Nov 2000Not visible in spring
Capex (autumn only)Business fixed investment grew about 21%, 15%, 8% annualised in Q1–Q3 2000, then fell about 1.5% in Q4Fed MPR Feb 2001Not visible in spring

Reading. In March–June 2000 nothing in the S&P 500's price, in earnings estimates or in credit said "bear market". The differences were structural: the leading index was already in a bear market, the Fed was still tightening, the 10y–2y curve was inverted, and the new-issue and margin channels that had fed the leaders were closing. The fundamental confirmation (bellwether warnings, credit spreads, capex) arrived only from late September 2000, which is when the S&P's second decline began.

2b. 2007: the first months as understood at the time (Oct 2007 – Jan 2008)
How deep, how fast (calc from [YH-SPX], [YH-DJI])
pointdateS&P 500from 9 Oct close
peak close2007-10-091,565.150
after Fed's 3rd easing step2007-10-311,549.38−1.0%
about 7 weeks (33 trading days)2007-11-261,407.22−10.1%
rally high2007-12-101,515.96−3.1%
year-end2007-12-311,468.36−6.2%
3 months2008-01-091,409.13−10.0%
15 weeks, closing low2008-01-221,310.50−16.3%
intraday low2008-01-231,270.05−18.9%
5 months, closing low2008-03-101,273.37−18.6%

So the "8% off after 6 weeks" point corresponds to about 20–26 Nov 2007. From there the index first rallied 7.7% in two weeks (Fed speeches), was only 6% off at year-end, and did not break the November low until 15 Jan 2008.

What the Fed was doing — already easing before the peak
  • 17 Aug 2007 discount rate −50bp; 18 Sep −50bp funds rate; 31 Oct −25bp; 11 Dec −25bp; 12 Dec TAF [FED-OMO] [PR0817] [STL]. Two easing steps came BEFORE the 9 Oct high.
  • 31 Oct statement called risks "roughly balance[d]"; four weeks later Kohn and Bernanke reversed that [MIN1031] [K1128] [B1129].
  • Market pricing on 5 Dec 2007: funds rate expected to bottom "just below 3 percent by the end of 2008" [GB1207]. Actual: 3.00% by 30 Jan 2008, 0–0.25% by Dec 2008.

NBER later dated the peak to December 2007 — the month in which the Fed staff said the economy "avoids recession" [NBER-P].

From memory only: consensus about 1,600–1,700, i.e. a gain of roughly 10% and no strategist in the Barron's panel forecasting a down year. Daily-report "buy the dip / healthy correction" quotes from Nov 2007: unverified (CNN Money archive unreachable).

Earnings at the time (Fed Greenbook, I/B/E/S data)
as ofstatementsource
5 Dec 2007Q3 2007 S&P 500 operating EPS "about 4 percent below year-ago levels"; financial-sector earnings "plummeted almost 30 percent"; non-financials +4%[GB1207]

Unverified (memory): Q4 2007 estimates started the quarter around +10–12% growth; calendar-2008 EPS estimates stayed near $100 (operating) into early 2008; Q3 2007 was the first y/y decline since 2002. The verified point is the split: the whole earnings decline sat in financials while "ex-financials" still looked fine — which is why it read as a sector problem.

What distinguished late Nov 2007 from an ordinary correction, in real time

Two ordinary 2007 corrections for contrast: Feb–Mar 2007 (−5.9%, new high in 7 weeks) and Jul–Aug 2007 (−9.4% on closes, −11.7% intraday, new high in 8 weeks) [YH-SPX]. Price depth alone did not separate November from August.

Margin debtrecord $416.4bn Jul 2007 (+34% in 6 months), −13% in August, never regained[FINRA]
Write-downs kept growingMerrill $5.5bn → $8.4bn in three weeks; two CEOs out in a week; loss estimate of about $200bn "had to be revised upward" in November[W-TL] [BRU]
Fed already cutting into a market at its high3 easing steps by 31 Oct[FED-OMO]
BreadthNYSE advance-decline line peaked June/July 2007: unverified—

Reading: what was different was not the size of the index drop but (a) leadership — financials, homebuilders, small caps and transports were already in their own bear markets before the index peaked; (b) credit never healed after August and was worse in Nov–Dec than in August; (c) the Fed was easing and it did not help funding markets; (d) the bad news kept being revised up. Against that: official forecasts (Fed staff, ex-financial earnings, Blue Chip 40% odds) still said "no recession", and the index rallied 7.7% on Fed speeches.

3. Bear-market rallies

3.1 Count, size and length inside each bear (computed)

Method: zig-zag on daily closes; a rally is counted once the index has risen the stated percentage from a closing low, and ends at the highest close before a fall of the same percentage [CALC]. Your own counts will differ with the threshold.
