After the average 10%+ Nasdaq rally of 2000–02 the index then fell 25% on average to its next low; after each of the four S&P rallies of 2007–09 it fell 15% to 37%.

3.2 What the published research says about them
ClaimDetailSource and status
Rallies are the norm inside bear marketsGoldman Sachs (Peter Oppenheimer's team, note "Bear Market Anatomy – the path and shape of the bear market", April 2025): 19 global bear-market rallies since the 1980s, average length 44 days, MSCI AC World up 10–15% on average. Reason given: positioning is light in bear markets, so small changes in news have large price effects[GS-CD] (press report of the note, opened). The note itself and Goldman's own article returned 403
What Goldman lists for a sustained bottomAttractive valuations, extreme negative positioning, policy support, and a slowing in the rate at which the macro data deteriorate[GS-CD]
Three types of bear marketStructural: about −60%, about a decade to recover fully. Cyclical: about −30%, about two years long, about five years to recover. Event-driven: about −30%, about eight months, about a year to recover. Oppenheimer on 2008: a structural bear with six strong rallies before the trough[GS-SEARCH]: search-result summaries of the Goldman article and of Bloomberg, 6 May 2025; pages not opened. Treat as unverified detail
Count in Goldman's Sept 2022 "Bear Repair" noteNot retrievedunverified
1930sAbout half a dozen double-digit rallies inside the 1929–32 decline (Callum Thomas, quoted in the same press report)[GS-CD]
3.3 What they looked like
FeatureEvidenceSource
Sharper than bull-market advancesThe 2000–02 bear contains 10 of the Nasdaq Composite's 20 largest one-day percentage gains since 1971 (3 Jan 2001 +14.2%, 5 Dec 2000 +10.5%, 5 Apr 2001 +8.9%, 18 Apr 2001 +8.1% …). The 2007–09 bear contains 7 of the S&P 500's 20 largest since 1950 (13 Oct 2008 +11.6%, 28 Oct 2008 +10.8% …)[CALC]
Big up days are a bear-market signatureS&P 500 up days of 3% or more: 17 in the 2000–02 bear, 23 in the 2007–09 bear, 2 in the whole 2003–07 bull (4.5 years), 18 in the 2009–20 bull (11 years). Nasdaq 2000–02: 66 up days of 3%+ and 85 down days of 3%+[CALC]
Volatility regimeNasdaq moved 5% or more on 27 days in 2000, against 7 days in all of 1990–99Fed Monetary Policy Report, Feb 2001 (section 2a)
Triggered by policy, not by earnings2001: surprise cuts of 3 Jan and 18 Apr. 2007–08: Kohn and Bernanke speeches (28–29 Nov 2007), TSLF and the Bear Stearns rescue (11–18 Mar 2008), short-sale ban and TARP proposal (18–19 Sep 2008), capital injections (13 Oct 2008), Citi rescue and QE1 (21–25 Nov 2008)Timelines in section 1
The real lows turned on earnings and bank-capital news10–15 Oct 2002: Yahoo, GE, Citigroup, GM, J&J results. 10–12 Mar 2009: Citigroup and Bank of America say they are profitableSection 1
Short and violent in a crisisMedian length of the ten 7%+ S&P rallies in 2007–09: 6 sessions; one was a single day (+11.6%)[CALC]
Led by the most beaten-down, at false and true lows alikeFirst leg off the true Mar 2009 low: KBW Bank index +86%, financials +91%, emerging markets +56%, staples +18%. So "junk leads" does not by itself mark a bear rallySection 7
Failed at or below a falling 200-day averageSee the table below[CALC]

Reading: every failed rally after the trend had turned (May 2001, Jan and Mar 2002, Aug 2002, May 2008, Aug 2008, Jan 2009) ended below, or no more than 2% above, a 200-day average that was still falling, with at most a dozen closes above it (none at all in five of the seven). But the first rally off the true October 2002 low looked the same (it never closed above the 200-day), and at the June 2009 high the 200-day was still falling. The 200-day filter kept a trader out of the failures; it did not identify the true low early. It confirmed in March 2003 (first close above on 21 Mar) and June 2009 (first close above on 1 Jun).

3.4 What separated a durable low from a bear rally (pointer to section 7)
SignBear ralliesDurable lows (Oct 2002 / Mar 2003; Mar 2009)Status
Fear and volumeRallies began from the fear and volume extremes (24 Jul 2002; 10 Oct and 20 Nov 2008)Final price low came on lower VIX and lower volume than the earlier lowsverified, section 7
CreditSpreads still widening through the rallies of Nov 2007, May 2008, Aug 2008Baa spread peaked 4 Dec 2008, three months before the low; in 2002 it peaked with the low and was lower at the Mar 2003 retestverified, sections 5 and 7
Non-confirmation at the last lowNew lows confirmed by growth indices, emerging markets and creditNasdaq-100 and SOX held their Nov 2008 lows on 9 Mar 2009; Nasdaq and SOX were 14% and 30% above their Oct 2002 lows at the Mar 2003 retestverified, section 7
Breadth thrustSingle 90% up-volume days failed repeatedly in 2008 (18 Mar, 13 Oct, 24 Nov, 16 Dec: reader-compiled list, unverified)Two 90% up-volume days inside one week (10 and 12 Mar 2009); Deemer breakaway-momentum signal 23 Mar 2009, the first since 1992. No thrust at all in 2002–03partly verified, section 7
Desmond / LowryRallies not preceded by a series of 90% downside days and not followed by a 90% upside day were suspect: he wrote in Feb 2002 that Sept 2001 had not been the bottomA 90% upside day (or two back-to-back 80% days) after a series of 90% downside days; in about half the historical cases it came within five sessions of the low[DESMOND]; the "half within five sessions" figure is from a search summary
EarningsTrailing EPS still falling fast (2001; 2008)EPS trough seven months before the Oct 2002 low; at the low in Mar 2009[SHILLER], section 4
New leadershipRallies led by the previous leaders bouncing (tech in 2001; financials and commodities in 2008)New leaders at 52-week highs within days or weeks: 15 future winners within seven weeks of 15 Oct 2002; NFLX on 17 Mar 2009section 8
PolicyRate cuts (2001, 2007–08)2009: capital, guarantees, asset purchases, accounting relief. 2002–03: nothing decisive; the low was on earnings and the retest on war newssections 1 and 5
Price shapeLower highs under a falling 200-day2009: +23% in 13 sessions and no retest; 2002–03: +21% in seven weeks, then a four-month retest to 3% above the lowsection 7

Honest limit: none of these was known to work in advance and the sample is two bears. Of the three earlier cases since 1960 of a 20% gain inside two weeks (after the 1987 crash, October 2008, November 2008), two had failed before March 2009 held (Quantifiable Edges, section 7).

4.2 When forward estimates started falling, relative to the price peak
BearWhat the record showsSource
2000Forecasts still being revised UP in July 2000, four months after the S&P peak. Bellwether warnings from 21 Sep 2000 (Intel, Apple, Dell, Home Depot, IBM, Nortel). Analysts cut year-ahead S&P estimates sharply only in Dec 2000 – Jan 2001: nine months after the March peak, three to four months after the September retest. Long-term growth forecasts barely moved even thenFed Monetary Policy Reports, July 2000 and Feb 2001; Greenspan, 13 Feb 2001 and 30 Aug 2002 (section 2a)
2007Revisions to year-ahead S&P 500 earnings were already negative through mid-November 2007, but about unchanged excluding financials. Q3 2007 EPS about −4% y/y (financials about −30%, the rest +4%); Q4 2007 projected about −20% y/y by 23 Jan 2008 (non-financials about +12%)Fed Greenbooks, 5 Dec 2007 and 23 Jan 2008 (section 2b)
Exact month in which the I/B/E/S forward-EPS series peaked (commonly put at about Sep–Oct 2000 and Oct–Nov 2007) and troughed (commonly about late 2001 / early 2002 and May 2009)Not retrievedunverified

Reading: in 2000 the estimates lagged the price peak by half a year or more; in 2007 they were falling at the peak but only in one sector, which is why it read as a sector problem. "Estimates are still rising" was true in spring 2000 and gave no protection.

For comparison, CAPE at the peaks before the corrections: 38.26 (Jul 1998), 23.14 (Apr 2011), 26.81 (May 2015), 32.62 (Sep 2018) [MU1].

Reading:

  • 2000 was a valuation peak on every measure. 2007 was not expensive on forward earnings (15.1x); the earnings were wrong.
  • The 2002 low was not cheap by history (14.1x forward, CAPE 22); the 2009 low was (10.4x, CAPE 13).
  • CAPE was 38 before the 1998 correction, so a high CAPE has also preceded mere corrections.

Yield-curve reading:

  • 2000: 10y–2y inverted from 2 Feb 2000 (seven weeks before the S&P peak) to 28 Dec 2000. 10y–3m inverted for two days in April and continuously from 7 Jul 2000 to 9 Feb 2001; it was at its most inverted to date (−0.59) on the day of the 1 Sep 2000 retest.
  • 2007: 10y–3m inverted from 17 Jul 2006 to 29 May 2007 and again briefly 20 Jul – 27 Aug 2007; 10y–2y from 8 Jun 2006 to 5 Jun 2007. By the October 2007 peak both had re-steepened to about +0.5/+0.6 because the Fed had begun cutting. The peak came AFTER the curve had dis-inverted.
  • Corrections: no inversion in 2011 or 2015; flat but positive in 2018 (inverted in 2019, after the correction); a brief, shallow 10y–2y inversion in May–July 1998.
  • 2022: not inverted at the peak (10y–2y inverted from July 2022, 10y–3m from October 2022).
  • 2026: 10y–3m was inverted from Oct to Dec 2024 and Feb to Oct 2025, and is now +1.06 with the 10-year near 5%: re-steepened as in 2007, but through rising long yields and a hiking Fed, not through cuts.

Credit reading:

  • 2007: the Baa spread made its low on 23 Feb 2007 (1.53), was 1.90 at the equity peak, 2.35 six weeks later and 2.67 after three months; it peaked at 6.16 on 4 Dec 2008. Credit turned eight months before equities and never retraced. High-yield spread levels for 2007–09 could not be retrieved; the commonly quoted record of about 21.8 points in mid-December 2008 is unverified.
  • 2000: the Baa spread made its low on 27 Jan 2000 (1.55), was 2.13 at the March peak and 2.54 at the September retest: about one point wider before the second decline began. Fed, Feb 2001: high-yield spreads "soared beginning in September" 2000. Peak 3.90 on 9–10 Oct 2002, the day of the equity low.
  • Corrections: spreads widened in all of them. 1998 was as violent as 2007 over the first three months (1.68 to 2.68). So widening alone does not mark a bear; what marked 2000 and 2007 was widening that started before the equity peak and did not retrace on equity rallies.
  • 2026: 1.46 at the peak, 1.52 on 18 Nov; high-yield spread 3.52% on 19 Nov (section 2b). Index-level credit has not confirmed, although single AI credits have (Oracle, CoreWeave: section 9).

Reading: banks were already tightening for two to three quarters as each major bear began (2000: 10.9, 24.6, 33.9; 2007: 7.5, 19.2, 32.1) and kept tightening for a year. In the corrections the reading was negative or near zero, except one quarter in late 1998. 2022–23 is the false positive: tightening as severe as 2000–01 with no recession. Now: 1.7.

Claims reading: the four-week average rose 18–19% within six months of the Sep 2000 and Oct 2007 peaks (313k to 371k; 312k to 370k) and 22% after July 1990. It was flat or lower after every correction peak (1998, 2011, 2015, 2018) and after Jan 2022. It gave nothing early in 1973. Now: about 200k.

5.3 Why the Fed cut hard and stocks still fell: the short version
2001–022007–09
Nature of the problemProfits and overbuilt capital, at a very high starting valuation; not a Fed-made downturnSolvency of leveraged intermediaries and falling collateral (housing); a run on short-term funding
What the cuts didSupported housing, autos and consumers; recession was mild and shortDid not reach borrowers: mortgage and Baa yields rose, banks tightened, securitisation shut
What the cuts could not doRestore telecom and tech capex, or justify 25x forward earnings while profits fell 54%Replace lost bank capital
What ended itEarnings stabilised (EPS trough Dec 2001 – Mar 2002), accounting scandals passed, valuations fell to 14x; low on earnings news, Oct 2002Capital injections, guarantees, asset purchases, stress tests, accounting relief; banks reporting profits, Mar 2009

Full evidence and sources follow in 5.4 (2001–02) and 5.5 (2007–09).

Three rallies of roughly 20% in the S&P and 40% in the Nasdaq, each failing at a lower high.

A3.3 What contemporaries and the literature say
ReasonEvidenceSource
It was not a Fed-made downturn, so easing could not simply reverse itBIS: the slowdown did not appear to have been triggered by monetary tightening; it was led by a sharp fall in US profits, followed by massive cuts in inventories and capital spendingBIS 72nd AR, ch. I and II (paraphrase)
Capital overhang from the boomFOMC, Aug 2001: "the emergence of substantial excess capacity in many industries, notably in high-tech facilities." Mar 2001: "a substantial volume of planned investment was being postponed, if not cancelled."FOMC minutes 2001-03-20, 2001-08-21
Size of the capex bustEquipment and software −8.5% in 2001 after +8.5% in 2000; communications equipment down more than 30%; capacity use lowest since 1983Fed MPR Feb 2002; FOMC minutes 2001-06-27
Overinvestment was real, concentrated in telecomTelephone-industry investment rate hit about 33% of capital stock in 2000 against a long-run 18%; that needed profit growth of roughly 9–10% a year in a mature industry. Industries that invested most in the late 1990s cut most in 2001McCarthy, NY Fed Current Issues, May 2004
Profit collapseNonfinancial profits fell at about 45% annualised in Q4 2000 and 25% a year through Q3 2001; profit share lowest since the early 1980s. FOMC statement 18 Apr 2001: "the persistent erosion in current and expected profitability"Fed MPR Feb 2002; FOMC statement 2001-04-18
Rate cuts did not ease financial conditions as usualBIS: long rates dipped then rose again, the dollar appreciated and equity prices kept weakening. FOMC, Aug 2001: some members felt financial conditions were giving less stimulus than the cuts implied. Poole, Jun 2001: long rates had not fallen since DecemberBIS 72nd AR ch. I (paraphrase); FOMC minutes 2001-06-27, 2001-08-21
Credit tightened against the cutsCorporate default rate nearly tripled in 2001 and passed its 1991 peak; business loans shrank 4.25%; banks kept tightening into 2002; IPO and venture funding stayed depressedFed MPR Feb 2002
Valuation starting pointS&P 500 P/E went from about 15 to nearly 30 between 1995 and 2000 (Greenspan). In mid-2002 the BIS still judged forward multiples high relative to historyGreenspan, Jackson Hole 2002; BIS 72nd AR ch. VI (paraphrase)
The market kept pricing a recovery that slippedBIS: the April 2001 rally rested on belief that easing would forestall recession; over the summer, earnings and macro data ended that belief. FOMC, Aug 2001: recovery timing "had again been pushed back"BIS 72nd AR ch. VI (paraphrase); FOMC minutes 2001-08-21
2002 was a trust shock, not a rates problemEnron, WorldCom and others eroded trust in reported profits; BIS warned this could weigh further on valuations. Funds rate was unchanged at 1.75% throughout the Jan–Oct 2002 legBIS 72nd AR, introduction (paraphrase); Fed rate table
Where the cuts did workConsumer spending +3% in 2001, autos and housing strong, record new-home sales, heavy mortgage refinancing. BIS: resilient household demand, helped by house prices, offset the corporate bustFed MPR Feb 2002; BIS 72nd AR ch. II (paraphrase)
The Fed's own framingGreenspan 2002: "no low-risk, low-cost, incremental monetary tightening exists that can reliably deflate a bubble"; the recession was "relatively mild". Greenspan 2004: the aim was to "mitigate the fallout when it occurs"; the Fed was "unusually aggressive in the initial stages of the recession of 2001"Greenspan speeches 2002-08-30, 2004-01-03
Later Fed assessmentFerguson 2005: asset-price-bust recessions are not necessarily costlier; 2001 "was shallow" thanks to sound banks and fast easingFerguson speech 2005-01-12
BIS framing in mid-2001A cycle of credit growth and technology optimism, then overinvestment, ending in a profit collapse feeding back into share prices: a harder landing even without prior inflationBIS 71st AR, conclusion (paraphrase)

Summary. The economy got the mild recession the cuts were aimed at (eight months, March to November 2001). Equities did not get a floor, because their problem was earnings and capital that had been overbuilt at very high valuations. Cheap money supported housing and consumers, not telecom capex or a 30x multiple. The index bottomed eleven months after the recession ended, on earnings news (Yahoo, GE, banks, 10–15 Oct 2002), not on a Fed action.

Academic work not retrieved (unverified): Kliesen, "The 2001 Recession: How Was It Different and What Developments May Have Caused It?" (St. Louis Fed Review, Sept/Oct 2003) — the URL redirected to an index page.

The cuts did not reach borrowers (data)
measureSep 2007mid-2008late 2008source
Fed funds target4.752.000–0.25[FED-OMO]
30-yr mortgage rate6.31 (13 Sep)6.63 (24 Jul 2008)6.46 (30 Oct); 5.10 (31 Dec, after QE1)[FRED-MORT]
Moody's Baa yield6.597.15 (Aug)9.21 (Nov)[FRED-BAA]
TED spread1.68 (18 Sep)0.89 (19 May)4.58 (10 Oct)[FRED-TED]
Banks tightening C&I standards, net %7.5 (Q3)55.4–57.783.6 (Q4)[FRED-SLOOS]
ABCP outstanding, $bn964 (19 Sep)about 778 (Sep 2008)696 (22 Oct)[FRED-ABCP]

Mortgage rates only fell when the Fed bought MBS directly (25 Nov 2008), not when it cut the funds rate.

Reasons, with sources
  1. Solvency, not just liquidity. Losses sat on thinly capitalised balance sheets. Investment banks ran up to 40:1 — "less than a 3% drop in asset values could wipe out a firm" [FCIC p.xix]. Citi and Merrill wrote down $23.8bn and $24.7bn by end-2007; BofA $9.7bn, Morgan Stanley $10.3bn, JPM $5.3bn, Bear $2.6bn [FCIC p.256]. Global write-downs about $500bn by end-Aug 2008 [BIS2]. A lower policy rate does not replace lost capital. BIS: stage 1 was about funding liquidity, stage 2 about solvency [BIS2].
  2. The credit channel was broken. Bernanke, 13 Jan 2009: the offset from rate cuts "has been incomplete" because of wider spreads, tighter standards and dysfunctional credit markets; "concerns about capital, asset quality, and credit risk continue to limit the willingness of many intermediaries"; securitisation markets shut except government-guaranteed mortgages; liquidity provision "is no panacea" [B0113].
  3. A run on the shadow banking system. Gorton–Metrick: the panic was a run on repo; falling collateral values and rising haircuts left the banking system insolvent in effect [GM]. Tri-party repo had grown from $800bn (2002) to $2.8trn (early 2008) [FCIC p.284]. Overnight repo's share of investment-bank funding roughly doubled 2000–2007 [BRU].
  4. Self-reinforcing deleveraging. Brunnermeier: loss spiral plus margin spiral (leveraged holders must sell as prices fall and as haircuts rise), the lending channel, runs, and network/counterparty effects turned mortgage losses of a few hundred billion dollars into about $8trn of lost US stock-market wealth (Oct 2007–Oct 2008) [BRU].
  5. Housing kept falling. Case-Shiller 20-city (SA) peaked Apr 2006; −7.6% by Oct 2007; −30.9% by Mar 2009; trough Mar 2012 (−33.9%). National index peak Feb 2007, trough Feb 2012 (−26.0%). Starts 2.273m (Jan 2006) → 0.478m (Apr 2009) [FRED-CS] [FRED-HOUST]. Collateral values fell through the whole easing cycle.
  6. Earnings collapsed. Q3 2007 −4% y/y, Q4 2007 about −20% y/y on financial losses [GB1207] [GB0108]. (Q4 2008 as-reported S&P EPS negative: unverified, S&P file not retrievable.)
  7. Fed insiders said so at the time. Fisher, 18 Mar 2008: liquidity measures would help more than further rate cuts [MIN0318]. March 2008 minutes: repo funding against agency and private MBS harder to get, larger haircuts, missed margin calls [MIN0318].
  8. What did work was balance-sheet policy: capital injections (13–14 Oct 2008), debt guarantees, asset purchases (25 Nov 2008, 18 Mar 2009), stress tests with a capital backstop (7 May 2009), and accounting relief (2 Apr 2009) [STL]. The final low came between the announcements, not after a rate cut.

Window dressingBear's balance sheet about 12% lower at quarter-end than average month-endp.281
  • 2004 SEC net-capital rule debate: on 28 Apr 2004 the SEC let the largest broker-dealers use internal models (CSE programme). Blinder, Coffee, Stiglitz and others blamed it for leverage rising from about 12–15:1 to 30–40:1. Rebuttal (Sirri 2009; GAO): the rule never capped holding-company leverage, and leverage was as high in 1998 (Bear 35:1, Goldman 31.6:1, Merrill 30:1, Lehman 28:1; five-firm average 27:1) [W-NCR].
  • Repo / short-term funding: tri-party repo $800bn (2002) → $1.7trn (2005) → $2.4trn (2007) → $2.8trn (early 2008) [FCIC p.284]. Pre-crisis repo haircuts (Treasuries about 0.25%, MBS about 5%): unverified — the FCIC passage exists but its figures could not be read.
  • ABCP: peak $1,225.7bn on 8 Aug 2007; $774.5bn at end-2007; $696bn 22 Oct 2008; $487bn end-2009; $348bn end-2010 [FRED-ABCP]. Average maturity fell from about 31 to about 23 days by mid-Sep 2007 [FCIC p.251–252].
  • SIVs: 36 vehicles with almost $400bn on the eve of the crisis; Moody's was still describing them as calm on 20 Jul 2007 [FCIC p.252].
  • CDOs: nearly $700bn of mortgage-related CDOs issued 2003–2007 [FCIC p.129].
  • Monolines: MBIA + Ambac guaranteed $265bn of mortgage securities and structured products; ACA had under $700m capital against $69bn of CDS on CDOs; cut to CCC 19 Dec 2007; MBIA/Ambac lost AAA on 5 Jun 2008 [FCIC p.276–277] [STL].
  • AIG Financial Products: $2.7trn notional derivatives book; $379bn of "regulatory capital" CDS for banks by 2007; $79bn of CDS on super-senior multisector CDOs; first Goldman collateral call 26 Jul 2007 [FCIC p.140, p.243]. Wikipedia: CDS on $441bn of AAA-rated securities, $57.8bn subprime-related; total support $182.3bn [W-AIG]. ($533bn total CDS notional: unverified.)

7. What marked the final lows

Notes on method for sections 7 and 8:

  • [Y] = Yahoo Finance chart API, daily bars, fetched this session and computed by script (scripts are in the scratchpad: yfetch.js, lead.js, rng.js, idx.js, misc.js, vol.js). Yahoo closes are split-adjusted (not dividend-adjusted); "nominal" = price at the time, rebuilt from Yahoo's own split events (these include spin-off factors for a few names, e.g. GE, EBAY, XLF, so nominal is approximate there). Delisted tickers have no Yahoo history and are listed as not verified.
  • "First 52-week high" = first close above the highest close of the prior 252 sessions. After a 50–80% crash this is a late, strict marker; IBD-style base breakouts come earlier. Use it to rank who recovered first, not as the buy point.
  • Yahoo's volume for ^GSPC and ^NYA is the same series (NYSE volume; composite from the mid-2000s). It matches the known records (2.78bn on 24 Jul 2002, 11.46bn on 10 Oct 2008).

Leadership of the first leg, 9 Mar to 30 Jun 2009 ([Y], price): KBW Bank index +86%, XLF +91%, KBE +84%, FXI +62%, EWZ +60%, EEM +56%, Nasdaq +45%, XLY +43%, XLI +43%, XHB +43%, XLB +42%, XLK +38%, SOX +38%, S&P +36%, XLE +24%, XLU +23%, XLV +20%, XLP +18%. That is the "junk rally": the groups hit last and hardest bounced most; defensives lagged. Day-one detail (CNBC, 10 Mar 2009): regional banks +16%, REITs +14%, homebuilders +12%, semis +8%, oil service +6%.

D2.2 2000–02: individual defiers ([Y], price)

"Low" here = lowest close on or after 1 Mar 2000, i.e. as the Nasdaq was peaking. Checked back to Jan 1999: for several names this was a retest of a slightly lower low made a few months earlier — NVR $38.00 on 27 Oct 1999, Lennar / Horton 21 Oct 1999, Toll 25 Oct 1999, UnitedHealth 13 Oct 1999, Tenet 17 Aug 1999, Philip Morris $18.94 on 14 Feb 2000, Hershey 28 Jan 2000. For Berkshire, Pulte, Lockheed (close), Northrop (2 Mar), General Dynamics (7 Mar), Ball (2 Mar) and Constellation the March 2000 low was the low.

Pattern: the "old economy" made its lows between October 1999 and the week the Nasdaq peaked (2–14 Mar 2000: Berkshire, Pulte, Lockheed, Northrop, General Dynamics, Ball, Constellation at their lows; NVR, Lennar, Toll, Horton, UnitedHealth, Philip Morris, Tenet, Hershey at successful retests) and turned up as the Nasdaq turned down. Most of these defiers then topped between March and July 2002 and fell 20–45% in the final leg — being a bear-market winner did not protect a stock in the last three months. Fund proxies confirm: REIT fund peak 12 Apr 2002, small-value and housing funds 3 May 2002, defence fund 14 May 2002, gold miners (XAU) 28 May 2002, Korea (KOSPI) 18 Apr 2002.

Commodities: gold futures closing low $255.1 on 15 Feb 2001, $316.5 at the Oct 2002 stock low, $379 by 4 Feb 2003; crude low $17.45 on 15 Nov 2001, $37.83 on 12 Mar 2003 (war premium) ([Y] front-month futures).

Relative winners ([Y], price): Dollar Tree +60.8% in 2008 (new 52-week high on 25 Nov 2008), Wal-Mart +17.9%, AutoZone +16.3% (52-week high 6 Feb 2009; $150 on 9 Mar 2009 vs $89 on 20 Nov 2008), Ross Stores +16.3%, General Mills +6.6%, McDonald's +5.6%, O'Reilly (low 27 Oct 2008, 52-week high 2 Jan 2009), Strayer (high 28 Nov 2008). But the defensives broke in the last leg: from 20 Nov 2008 to 9 Mar 2009 XLU −13%, XLP −12%, XLV −7% while XLK was flat (+0.3%); P&G, J&J, Coca-Cola, Hershey, Campbell, Clorox, General Mills and Exxon all made their bear-market closing lows on 5–9 Mar 2009. "Nowhere to hide" was true of equities after Sep 2008; the only real hedges were Treasuries (to 18–19 Dec 2008), the yen and, from November, gold.

India: Sensex closing low was 9 Mar 2009 (8,160) but its intraday low was 27 Oct 2008 (7,697). Japan: Nikkei closing low 10 Mar 2009 (7,055), intraday low 28 Oct 2008 (6,995). Germany and the UK made their lows with the US (DAX 6 Mar, FTSE 3 Mar 2009).

Reading: from 20 Nov 2008 to 9 Mar 2009 the S&P lost another 10.1% and the Dow 13.3%, but almost all of it was banks, GE/industrials and the defensives that had held up before. Tech, semis, retail, emerging markets, China, commodities, gold and credit had all stopped going down between late October and late December 2008.

Leaders of the next bull (D3)

Column notes: prices from [Y]. "Low" = lowest close in the bear window (nominal price at the time); the window is 1 Mar 2000 – 30 Jun 2003 for the 2003 bull and 9 Oct 2007 – 30 Jun 2009 for the 2009 bull, so for homebuilders and UNH the slightly lower Oct 1999 lows (D2.2) are outside it. "Adj closes" are split-adjusted, on the three index-low dates. "Above own low" = how far the stock stood above its own bear low on the day of the index's final low/retest. "First 52-wk high" = first 52-week closing high after the index low (strict marker, see top). "Peak" = highest close to end-2007 (2003 bull) or end-2011 (2009 bull) and the multiple of the bear low.

Order of emergence (first 52-week closing highs, [Y]):

  • Before the index low: DLTR (25 Nov 2008), ORLY (2 Jan 2009), AZO (6 Feb 2009).
  • In the 13 weeks after the 12 Mar 2009 follow-through-type day: NFLX (17 Mar), NTES (3 Apr), HANS (23 Apr), MED (18 May), FFIV (11 Jun).
  • July 2009 wave (off the only pullback, −7% into 10 Jul): TSCO and ROST (13 Jul), SWKS (15 Jul), AMZN, PCLN, NEU (20 Jul), SBUX (22 Jul), CMG and F (23 Jul), BIDU (24 Jul), CTRP (3 Aug), CTSH (4 Aug).
  • September 2009 wave: AAPL and ULTA (4 Sep), GS and MELI (9 Sep), V (10 Sep), GOOG (11 Sep), CRUS (15 Sep), FOSL and SOHU (16 Sep), CRM (17 Sep), FCX and MA (22 Sep), ISRG (30 Sep). S&P 500's own 52-week high: 12 Oct 2009.
D3.3 The pattern (O'Neil / IBD) and what the data says
  • The O'Neil/IBD teaching — the real new leaders are the first stocks to break out of sound bases in roughly the first 3 to 13 weeks after a follow-through day, and many of them held well above their own lows while the index made its final low — is stated here from general knowledge; the IBD/O'Neil texts and IBD's follow-through-day dates could not be retrieved (investors.com blocked): unverified as a citation.
  • The data above supports it. 2003: 15 of the eventual big winners made 52-week highs within 7 weeks of 15 Oct 2002, and every one of them had made its own low 12–31 months earlier; at the March 2003 retest they stood 60% to 1,900% above their lows. 2009: the big growth winners (AMZN, PCLN, BIDU, NFLX, CMG, CRM, MELI, FCX, GS) made their lows between 27 Oct and 10 Dec 2008 and were 27–115% above them on 9 Mar 2009, with the S&P 10% below its November close.
  • Two other kinds of winner behaved differently and need different handling: (1) late-stage casualties that bottomed with or after the index and then ran hardest in percentage terms (2009: LULU, ULTA, DECK, FOSL, LVS, WYNN, banks; 2003: AAPL, ISRG, TASR) — no relative-strength clue at the low; (2) new issues that came public late in the bear or early in the bull (NFLX May 2002, JBLU Apr 2002, CME Dec 2002, GOOG Aug 2004, CTRP Dec 2003; TSLA Jun 2010).
  • Bear-market winners are not automatically next-bull leaders: homebuilders led both the 2000–02 bear and the 2003–05 bull, but the 2000–02 defensive winners (tobacco, defence, HMOs topping in mid-2002) and the 2008 trade-down winners (WMT, MCD, GIS) lagged afterwards (2009: WMT −4.7%, MCD +0.4%).

9. AI boom 2023–2026 against the dot-com bubble

The dot-com side of the comparison (valuation in March 2000, what happened to Cisco, Intel, Lucent and Nortel, Ritter's IPO data, telecom capex and vendor financing) is in section 4.4. Legend as there: "derived", "secondary", "unverified". Nearly all 2026 company figures come from secondary trackers that cite filings; the primary documents opened were Nvidia's Q3 release, FactSet's 13 Nov report, two FOMC statements, FINRA, Ritter and the J.P. Morgan guide.

Depreciation and useful lives [YT6] (secondary)

  • Michael Burry, X post 10 Nov 2025: hyperscalers will understate depreciation by $176bn over 2026–28; by 2028 Oracle's earnings overstated 26.9%, Meta's 20.8%. No method published.
  • Useful lives: Microsoft servers 4 → 6 years (FY2023, +$3.0bn net income); Alphabet → 6 years (2023, +$3.0bn); Meta → 5.5 years (2025, about −$2.9bn depreciation); Oracle 5 → 6 (FY2025, +$573m); Amazon 5 → 6 (2024), then a subset back 6 → 5 (2025, −$1.0bn net income, citing AI-driven change).
  • Depreciation growth: Meta Q2 2026 $6.00bn vs $4.28bn (+40%); Alphabet H1 2026 $13.59bn vs $9.49bn (+43%).
  • Counter-evidence: median H100 on-demand rental $3.39/hour in late Sept 2026, up 14% y/y — old chips are not yet being dumped.
  • Sparkline (Oct 2025): Mag-7 depreciation could go from about $150bn to $400bn in five years [SPK].

C2.7 What happened in Oct–Nov 2026 (dated chronology)

Macro backdrop, which differs from the brief's assumptions: the Fed is raising rates (first hike 16 Sep 2026 to 3.75–4.00%, vote 12–0 [FED2]); a US–Iran war has been running since about Feb–Mar 2026 with Brent $90–113 [AP1][CN2]; the 10-year Treasury hit 5.35% on 7 Oct, highest since April 2002 [CN1]; mortgage rates are the highest since 2000 [CN4].

Stated triggers, per the coverage: (1) AI financing strain — negative FCF, heavy bond and equity supply, widening spreads; (2) rising rates and a hiking Fed; (3) the midterm result and data-centre opposition; (4) Nvidia's CEO scandal; (5) the Anthropic IPO draining liquidity (Dan Ives's note calling it a perfect storm) [YF1][RT1]. Notably the sell-off came with earnings estimates still rising and the forward P/E already at a three-year low.

Other dated views (all via [WK-AI], secondary): Ray Dalio, 27 Jan 2025 (very similar to dot-com); Sam Altman, Aug 2025 (a bubble exists); Jamie Dimon, 9 Oct 2025 (AI is real, some money will be wasted, higher odds of a meaningful drop than priced); Julien Garran, Oct 2025 (a bubble 17 times the size of dot-com, by his estimate); Kansas City Fed's Schmid, 25 Sep 2026 (is AI too big to fail?). Baird's Antonelli, 20 Nov 2026: the idea that AI spending can go on without limit is coming to an end, but he does not think the bull market from Oct 2022 is over [AP1]. Bezos's "good bubble" remark and any 2026 update from Goldman or Morgan Stanley: unverified.

The one-paragraph reading of the data. On price-to-forward-earnings, 2026 is nowhere near 2000 (about 17x vs 25x, and the tech sector is below its long-run average). On the capital cycle it is beyond 2000: capex exceeds operating cash flow, free cash flow has turned negative at three of the four largest builders, funding has shifted to bonds, leases and equity issuance, and the weakest credits (Oracle, CoreWeave) are being repriced toward distress. The 2000–02 record says the second fact matters more for the leaders than the first: Cisco and Intel were highly profitable market leaders, and their earnings still fell 88% (Intel) and 138% (Cisco, to a loss) within a year once customers stopped ordering. This sell-off has begun while consensus estimates are still rising [FS1]; whether estimates were also still rising in spring 2000 was not checked.

10. Two-scenario checklist material

Scenario A = correction inside a bull market (1998, 2011, 2015–16, 2018). Scenario B = start of a major bear (2000, 2007; 1973 and 2022 as further cases). Numbers are from the computed tables in sections 2.0 and 5.2 [CALC] unless another section is named. The 1998, 2011, 2015–16 and 2018 episodes were not researched for events here (other files in this folder cover them); for those columns only the computed data is used.

Timing class: leading = visible before the index peak; coincident = within about three months after it; lagging = later. Reliability is my reading of this sample (four corrections, four bears) and of the sources; it is not a tested statistic.

10.2 Tally for Nov 2026, as far as the data goes
LeaningIndicators
B-like (bear start)1 leadership divergence; 4 Fed hiking; 10 margin debt peaked before the index; 11 supply; 15 first cracks in the lead sector's capex
A-like (correction)5 index-level credit spreads; 6 bank standards; 7 jobless claims
Neutral or uninformative3 curve (ambiguous); 8 estimates rising; 9 valuation mixed; 12 depth; 13 200-day; 14 volatility; 16 consensus

This is a description of the readings, not a forecast. The closest historical mix (tightening Fed, high CAPE, divergent leadership, no stress yet in credit, claims or bank standards, estimates still rising) is spring 2000 and January 2022, not autumn 2007. In 2000 the credit and labour confirmation arrived six to nine months later; in 2022 the labour confirmation never came and the bear ended at −25%.

11.1 Leveraged index ETFs through the 2007–09 bear (computed)

The trader uses 2x and 3x index ETFs. The 2x funds traded through the whole bear (SSO and QLD from 21 Jun 2006). The first 3x S&P fund (SPXL) began trading on 5 Nov 2008, so it covers only the last four months; UPRO started 25 Jun 2009 and TQQQ 11 Feb 2010 (first dates in the caller's cache). Points: a 2x fund lost about 85% over the bear (it needs about +550% to recover); the +49% it made in the Nov 2008 – Jan 2009 rally was erased within two months; and SSO was still 20% under water on the day the index was back at its old high, because of daily rebalancing through a volatile decline. The 3x fund lost 71% in four months in which the index lost 29%, and gained 71% in the bear rally in between. A 3x fund's path through the full 55% decline (twenty-three 3% up days and thirty-two 3% down days in the S&P) cannot be observed; the direction of the effect is the same and larger.

11.2 Smaller points from the research
PointDetailWhere
The 2000 top was a process with two index peaksS&P 500 record 24 Mar 2000, retest to within 0.4% on 1 Sep 2000; the Dow had peaked on 14 Jan 2000, the Nasdaq on 10 Mar. The decline that did not recover started only after the September retest, when bellwether warnings began1a, 2a
The average stock rose in the first two years of the 2000–02 bearValue Line Arithmetic (equal-weighted, about 1,700 stocks): +9.6% in 2000, +10.9% in 2001, −17.1% in 2002. Small value +22% and +14%; REITs +26% and +14%8
In 2007–09 it was the oppositeEqual-weight S&P −60.0% against −56.8% cap-weighted; value did not protect; correlations went to one after Sept 20088
Winners of a bear are not safe in its last legThe 2000–02 defiers (homebuilders, HMOs, tobacco, defence, REITs, small value) topped March–July 2002 and fell 20–45% into October 2002. The 2008 defensives (staples, utilities, health care) fell 7–13% from 20 Nov 2008 to 9 Mar 2009 while technology was flat8
The index's last leg can be narrowFrom 20 Nov 2008 to 9 Mar 2009 the S&P fell another 10.1%, nearly all of it banks, GE/industrials and defensives; tech, semis, retail, emerging markets, commodities, gold and credit had stopped falling7, 8
Being right on value was early by monthsBuffett's "Buy American. I Am." (mid-Oct 2008, S&P about 940): the low was about 28% lower, five months later. Grantham's "Reinvesting When Terrified" (4–10 Mar 2009) was within days7
A careful bear missed the 2009 low by demanding index confirmationHussman saw no favourable divergences on 9 Mar 2009; the divergences existed in the Nasdaq-100, SOX, emerging markets, credit and VIX7
Both official recession calls were lateNBER announced the March 2001 start on 26 Nov 2001 (the month the recession ended) and the Dec 2007 start on 1 Dec 2008 (S&P −8.9% that day)1a, 1b
Sell-the-news on rescue policy was the rule while the bear lastedTARP passed 3 Oct 2008: −1.4% that day and −18% the next week. Geithner plan 10 Feb 2009: −4.9%. ARRA signed 17 Feb 2009: −4.6%. Rate cut 11 Dec 2007: −2.5%1b
The 2002 leg had no macro causeFunds rate flat at 1.75%, recession over since Nov 2001, trailing EPS rising from March 2002; the S&P fell 34% from 4 Jan to 9 Oct 2002 on accounting scandals (Enron, WorldCom, Tyco) and valuation1a, 4
Things new in 2026 with no clean precedent in either bearA hiking Fed with the 10-year above 5% during an AI capex boom; hyperscalers issuing equity; the largest two IPOs ever inside five months; a sell-off that began with the forward P/E already at a three-year low and estimates rising9
One 2026 parallel to 2000 that is easy to missIn 2000 the turn in the leaders came from their customers' financing (dot-coms and CLECs running out of cash, vendor loans going bad), not from end demand. In 2026 the first stress is also in financing (negative free cash flow, bond spreads for Oracle and CoreWeave, supplier investments in customers) while reported demand is still strong4.4, 9

  • Robertson's and Soros/Druckenmiller's own words and Quantum's year-to-date loss; the dates rest on Wikipedia's citations of CNNfn/NYT/WaPo.
  • Kozlowski's indictment date (4 Jun 2002); the 3 Jun resignation is verified.
  • Wall Street strategists' year-end 2000 and 2001 S&P targets.
  • First Call / Thomson pre-announcement counts and the path of 2001 EPS estimates.
  • NYSE margin-debt dollar levels.
  • US market reaction to the Argentine default and to the JGTRRA signing.
  • The 13 Mar 2000 Nasdaq move (−2.6%) is from Wikipedia only.
  • Dow and S&P intraday extremes other than those quoted from AP reports.
Block B: 2007–09 and leverage
  1. Wall Street strategists' 2008 year-end S&P targets (Dec 2007 surveys) and any "buy the dip / correction" quotes from Nov 2007 daily market reports.
  2. Earnings-estimate trajectory: Q4 2007 starting near +10–12%; 2008 EPS estimates staying high; Q3 2007 as "first negative quarter since 2002"; Q4 2008 as-reported EPS.
  3. NYSE-only margin debt levels ($278.5bn, $381.4bn, $173bn) and the "+75% in five months" claim; margin debt as % of market cap.
  4. NYSE advance-decline line peak (June/July 2007).
  5. HFR industry assets ($1.93trn → about $1.4trn), Q4 2008 redemptions (about $150bn), gates count; HFRI convertible-arbitrage 2008 return; Lehman prime-brokerage freeze.
  6. Goldman Global Alpha and AQR August 2007 losses; $3bn injection into Global Equity Opportunities.
  7. Equity-only fund outflows for Oct 2008, Feb–Mar 2009 and July 2002.
  8. Event details: ECB EUR 94.8bn exact figure (sources say about EUR 95bn); Chrysler / Alliance Boots loan postponements; Citi's Nov 2007 write-down size; Citi and Merrill Q4 2007 results (15–17 Jan 2008); Peloton collapse date; Volkswagen squeeze dates and Porsche's 74.1%; AIG Q4 2008 loss $61.7bn; Wells Fargo 9 Apr 2009 pre-announcement; FASB hearing date 12 Mar 2009; uptick-rule talk 10 Mar 2009; that the 21 Nov 2008 rally was caused by the Geithner report; AIG $533bn CDS notional; SCAP adverse-scenario loss total.
  9. Causes of several large single-day moves where only the price is verified (19 Oct 2007, 7 Nov 2007, 23 Jan 2008 reversal, 15 Oct 2008).
  10. High-yield spread levels for 2007–2009.
Block C: AI and dot-com
  • S&P tech sector forward P/E and weight at March 2000; top-10 weight and names in 2000; best-sourced Nasdaq-100 P/E for 2000.
  • Exact peak-to-trough percentages for Cisco, Intel, Microsoft, Oracle, Sun, EMC, Qualcomm, Corning (I have year-end market caps and Wikipedia's "80%" for Cisco only); Microsoft regaining its peak in 2016; Cisco "130x".
  • FY2000 revenue / net income for Microsoft, Oracle, Sun, Dell, EMC, Qualcomm, Nortel.
  • WorldCom $41bn debt / $107bn assets; JDSU $56bn loss; Motorola–Telsim and Alcatel vendor loans; fibre miles laid; US carrier capex decline 2001–03. Nortel and Cisco vendor-finance totals rest on one secondary blog.
  • Market breadth statistics for 1999–2000.
  • Magnificent-7 and Nasdaq-100 forward P/E now; consensus forward P/E for Nvidia; per-company capex for 2023 and 2024; Apple and xAI capex.
  • IMF GFSR October 2026 (not on the IMF listing page I opened; latest shown was April 2026, about the Middle East war); any 2026 Bank of England FPC statement; Bridgewater, BIS.
  • Meta Hyperion / Blue Owl terms; private-credit and data-centre ABS/CMBS totals; Nvidia's CoreWeave backstop.
  • Retail, options and leveraged-ETF activity; Ritter 2026 year-to-date IPO statistics.
  • Margin debt ÷ GDP uses an implied GDP figure, not BEA data; the NYSE-series figures ($278.5bn, $381bn) were not checked.
  • Nearly all 2026 company figures come from secondary trackers (Yield Theory, Walnut, ValueAdd VC, Leverage Shares) that cite filings. Primary documents I did open: Nvidia's Q3 release, FactSet's 13 Nov report, the two FOMC statements, FINRA, Ritter, the JPMorgan guide, and the Cisco / Intel / Lucent 10-Ks.
  • Internal inconsistencies noted above: drawdown size (−11.6% by arithmetic vs "10–10.7%" in press); Alphabet equity raised ($61.9bn vs $84.75bn); Apollo's 4.5% earnings yield vs FactSet's P/E; FactSet's "other 493" growth (34.7% vs 32.6% in the same article); Google Cloud "+103%" in one tracker looks high and was not checked.
Block D: lows and leaders
  1. IBD's own designation of follow-through days (15 Oct 2002, 17 Mar 2003, 12 Mar 2009) and its counts of failed follow-through days; O'Neil model-book and IBD leader lists. (Price/volume conditions on those dates are verified.)
  2. Lowry / Desmond: 90% downside and upside day dates for Jul–Oct 2002 and Mar 2003; Lowry's official 2008–09 list (the 2009 dates come from Quantifiable Edges for 10 and 12 Mar and otherwise from an unverified reader table); Desmond's real-time comments in early 2009 and afterwards; Buying Power / Selling Pressure readings.
  3. Zweig Breadth Thrust signals (Mar 2009; 2003), Whaley thrust, NDR thrust indicators.
  4. ICE BofA high-yield OAS peaks and levels (Dec 2008 ~21.8%, Oct 2002, Mar 2003, Mar 2009); LIBOR-OIS peak.
  5. NYSE new 52-week-low counts on 10 Oct 2008 (2,631 from a search snippet only), 20–21 Nov 2008, 6–9 Mar 2009, and all 2002–03 counts; % of stocks above 200-day/50-day averages at the Nov 2008, Mar 2009 and 2002–03 lows.
  6. Investors Intelligence bulls/bears, put/call ratios, magazine covers, Rydex, Conference Board confidence, BofA/Merrill fund-manager cash levels, ICI fund flows (only the TrimTabs $30bn figure via CNBC is sourced).
  7. Insider buy/sell ratio records (Jul 2002, Oct–Nov 2008, Mar 2009) beyond the two qualitative mentions.
  8. Exact date of Buffett's op-ed (16/17 Oct 2008), Doug Kass's "generational low" call, Bill Miller.
  9. Forward EPS / earnings-revision troughs (both cycles); ISM lows in 2001–03 and revised 2008 values; the 12 Mar 2009 mark-to-market hearing date.
  10. Russell 1000 Value vs Growth for calendar 2000, S&P 500 equal-weight returns 2000–02, S&P GICS sector index returns (SPDR ETFs and funds used as proxies), median-stock returns.
  11. Stocks with no Yahoo history: YHOO, SINA, BRCM, SNDK, OVTI, CHS, RYL, APOL, COCO, AET, DNA, STJ, HAR, IGT, DELL, ASKJ, XTO, X, TZOO, GMCR, RVBD, ARMH, OPEN, STEC, HGSI, DNDN, VMW, SWN, POT, MON. GE's nominal low ($5.73 vs Yahoo back-adjusted $5.87).

Other drops and the evidence on bottoming signals

18900 words.

Open the note

Other drops and the evidence base on bottoming signals

Research notes for the market-drops study. Written 20 Nov 2026. Part A profiles nine drops outside 2018 and the 2000/2007 bears. Part B is the evidence on bear rallies, bottoming signals, analog charts and intraday behaviour.

Read this first

  • Web search ran out. The session's search budget (200 calls, shared by the three helper agents and me) was used up early. Everything after that was checked by fetching known URLs and by calculation from the local daily files. Several primary documents could not be opened; they are listed in "Could not verify" at the end.
  • Three kinds of evidence, marked in the text:
  • a URL or a source tag in square brackets = read from that source;
  • (calc) = computed by a helper agent from Yahoo/FRED daily history;
  • (computed here) = computed by me from the project's own files in scripts/marketdrops/.data/raw (S&P 500 yahoo__GSPC.csv, Nasdaq yahoo__IXIC.csv, yahoo_SPY.csv, cboe_VIX.csv, cboe_VIX3M.csv). The scripts are in the session scratchpad and are throwaway; every definition is written out so you can recode it.
  • "Unverified" means I could not confirm it from a source I opened. Treat it as a lead.
  • High-yield spreads. FRED now serves only three years of the ICE BofA high-yield series, so older episodes use Moody's Baa minus 10-year Treasury [BAA] and the TED spread [TED] instead.
  • Index volume. Yahoo's S&P 500 index volume has bad prints (it shows 8 Aug 2011 at 0.64 times average, while SPY shows 3.1 times). Volume facts below use SPY from 1998; the follow-through-day counts use index volume and are approximate.

A1. 1987 crash

Cause. Prices had outrun earnings and rates were rising worldwide. On 14 Oct a House bill to end merger tax breaks and a bad trade deficit hit the market; the S&P fell over 9% that week [Carlson]. On 19 Oct portfolio-insurance selling met stale cash prices and a futures discount.

  • Portfolio insurers were about 40% of non-market-maker futures sales on 19 Oct; the top ten sellers were 50% [Carlson, citing the Brady Report].
  • Regulators disagreed on blame: the SEC and CME called it significant but not sufficient; the CFTC was sceptical [Carlson].

Price [Y]

DateS&P 500
Closing peak25 Aug 1987336.77 (intraday 337.89)
Friday before16 Oct282.70
Black Monday close19 Oct224.84 (−20.47%)
Intraday low20 Oct216.46 (close 236.83)
Closing low4 Dec 1987223.92 (intraday 221.24)
  • Depth −33.5% closing, −35.9% intraday. 55 days to the crash, 101 days to the closing low.
  • The old high was regained on 26 Jul 1989 [https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Monday_(1987)].
  • Nasdaq: 455.80 (27 Aug) to 291.90 (28 Oct), −36% [Y].

Fed. It had been tightening.

  • Discount rate +50bp to 6% on 4 Sep 1987, the first rise since 1984 [Richmond Fed 2006, read via a search summary].
  • 20 Oct, before the open: the one-sentence statement of readiness "to serve as a source of liquidity". The funds rate went from over 7.5% to about 7% that day [Carlson] and 6.5% by early November [https://www.frbsf.org/research-and-insights/publications/economic-letter/1997/10/the-october-87-crash-ten-years-later/].

Earnings and economy. EPS rose: $14.48 (1986), $17.50 (1987), $23.75 (1988) [EPS]. No recession [NBER].

Credit. TED spread 0.82 (25 Aug) → 2.26 (16 Oct) → peak 3.01 (20 Oct) → 2.38 (4 Dec) [TED]. Baa–10y peaked at 2.68 on 22 Oct [BAA].

Order of signs at the low

  1. Record week of selling, 14–16 Oct.
  2. VXO record close 150.19 on 19 Oct [https://macroption.com/vix-all-time-high]. The intraday high is disputed (172.79 on 20 Oct in one source, 152.48 on 19 Oct in another).
  3. 20 Oct: Fed statement, intraday price low, TED peak, futures halted, then corporate buyback announcements [Carlson].
  4. 21 Oct: +9.1% [Y].
  5. 26 Oct: −8.3% to 227.67, which held above the low [Y].
  6. 4 Dec: marginal closing undercut (0.9 points) on below-average volume, intraday low intact, TED lower than in October.

No breadth thrust followed in 1987–88 [BAM].

Retest. Yes. The closing low was undercut by 0.4% seven weeks later; the intraday low held.

What led afterwards. In 1988 the Russell 2000 rose 22.4% against 12.4% for the S&P [Y]. Sector detail unverified.

Timeline

DateEvent
25 Aug 1987S&P closing peak 336.77

19 Oct−20.5%; VXO closes 150.19

Sources: [Carlson] Fed FEDS 2007-13, federalreserve.gov/pubs/feds/2007/200713/200713pap.pdf · federalreservehistory.org/essays/stock-market-crash-of-1987 · newbagehot.yale.edu/docs/federal-reserves-response-1987-mark · Brady Report, sechistorical.org/collection/papers/1980/1988_0101_BradyRepo (not read directly).

---

A2. 1990: Kuwait invasion and recession

Cause. The economy was already weak: restrictive Fed in 1989–90, overbuilt commercial property, bank failures [https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_1990s_recession_in_the_United_States]. Iraq invaded Kuwait on 2 Aug 1990.

  • Oil averaged $17 in July, hit $28 on 6 Aug and peaked at $46 in mid-October [https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1990_oil_price_shock].
  • Recession July 1990 – March 1991 [NBER]. The market peak and the cycle peak fell in the same month.
  • Junk bonds: Drexel filed for bankruptcy on 13 Feb 1990 [https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drexel_Burnham_Lambert]. Thrift clean-up: federalreservehistory.org/essays/savings-and-loan-crisis.

Price [Y]

DateS&P 500
Closing peak16 Jul 1990368.95
First low23 Aug307.06 (−16.8%)
Second low27–28 Sep300.97 close
Final low11 Oct 1990295.46 (intraday 294.51)
  • Depth −19.9% closing, −20.4% intraday; 87 days. Sources argue over whether it counts as a bear market [https://globalfinancialdata.com/why-20-five-bear-markets-that-almost-occurred].
  • New closing high on 13 Feb 1991, four months after the low.
  • Nasdaq −30.7% (to 16 Oct).

Fed [FA]. One cut on 13 Jul 1990 (8.25% → 8.00%), before the invasion. Nothing during the oil spike. Then cuts from 29 Oct, 18 days after the low, down to 4.00% by 20 Dec 1991.

Earnings. EPS fell from $22.87 (1989) to $15.97 (1991), about −30% [EPS]. The earnings trough came well after the price low.

Credit. Stress peaked after the equity low: TED max 1.78 on 27 Dec 1990; Baa–10y max 2.45 on 12 Dec 1990 [TED, BAA].

Order of signs at the low

  1. VIX closing peak 36.47 on 23 Aug, at the first low [Y].
  2. Lower price lows on 28 Sep and 11 Oct with a lower VIX (33.98 on 11 Oct). Oil peaked in the same mid-October window.
  3. First post-invasion Fed cut on 29 Oct.
  4. Credit spreads peaked in December.
  5. Higher low in January 1991 (311.49 on 9 Jan), about 5% above the October low, with the VIX back at 36.20 on 14 Jan.
  6. Air war began 17 Jan 1991; S&P +3.7% that session, VIX down to 27.45 [Y].
  7. Breadth thrust on 5 Feb 1991 (10-day advance/decline ratio 2.17) [BAM].
  8. New high on 13 Feb.

Retest. The August low was undercut twice (September, October). The October low was never retested.

What led afterwards. Growth and Nasdaq: Nasdaq +57% from 16 Oct 1990 to 17 Apr 1991. From the 11 Oct close to 30 Dec 1991: Amgen +390%, Home Depot +271%, Microsoft +186% [Y].

Timeline

2 AugIraq invades Kuwait
23 AugFirst low 307.06; VIX peak 36.47
11 OctFinal low 295.46; oil peaks mid-October
29 OctFed resumes cutting
17 Jan 1991Air war starts; S&P +3.7%

13 FebNew closing high

---

A3. 1994: Fed tightening and the bond massacre

Cause. A pre-emptive tightening after the funds rate had sat at 3%. On 4 Feb 1994 the FOMC announced its first tightening since 1989 [https://www.federalreserve.gov/fomc/19940204default.htm]. Leveraged bond positions unwound worldwide.

Fed [FA]

DateMoveFunds target
4 Feb 1994+25bp3.25%
22 Mar+25bp3.50%
18 Apr+25bp3.75%
17 May+50bp4.25%
16 Aug+50bp4.75%
15 Nov+75bp5.50%
1 Feb 1995+50bp (last)6.00%
6 Jul 1995−25bp (first cut)5.75%

Bonds. 10-year yield 5.81% (3 Feb 1994) → peak 8.05% (7 Nov 1994) [https://fred.stlouisfed.org/series/DGS10]. Losses of about $1.5tn globally [https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_bond_market_crisis].

Price [Y]

DateS&P 500
Closing peak2 Feb 1994482.00
Hike day4 Feb469.81 (−2.3%)
Low4 Apr 1994438.92 (intraday 435.86)
Later higher lows24 Jun; 8 Dec442.80; 445.45
  • Depth −8.9% closing over 61 days. The rest of the year was a range of 438–477.
  • Nasdaq peaked later (18 Mar) and bottomed later (24 Jun), −13.7%.
  • New S&P closing high on 14 Feb 1995, two weeks after the last hike.

Earnings. EPS rose about 40% in 1994 [EPS]. No recession. This was a valuation squeeze with rising profits.

Credit. Calm in corporates: Baa–10y narrowed through the year [BAA]. Funding stress only late: Orange County filed on 6 Dec 1994 [https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Citron]; Mexico devalued on 20 Dec [https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_peso_crisis]; TED max 0.99 on 29 Dec [TED].

Order of signs at the low

  1. VIX 9.94 on 28 Jan, 15.25 on the hike day.
  2. VIX closing peak 23.87 on 4 Apr, the same day as the price low.
  3. Higher lows in June and December while bonds kept falling until 7 Nov.
  4. The December shocks produced only a higher low.
  5. Last hike 1 Feb 1995; new high 14 Feb.

No retest of the April low. No breadth-thrust signal in 1994–95 [BAM].

What led afterwards. 1995: S&P +34.1%, Nasdaq +39.9%, Intel about +78% [Y]. Broader sector leadership unverified.

Timeline

7 Nov10-year yield peaks at 8.05%
15 Nov+75bp

20 DecPeso devaluation
1 Feb 1995Final hike, to 6.00%
14 Feb 1995New closing high

---

A4. 1998: Russia and LTCM

Cause. On 17 Aug 1998 Russia devalued and defaulted on domestic debt [https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_Russian_financial_crisis]. A global flight to quality broke convergence trades.

  • LTCM lost 44% in August. On 23 Sep, 14 firms put in about $3.6bn; the Fed brokered the deal but lent nothing [https://www.federalreservehistory.org/essays/ltcm-near-failure].
  • The yen carry trade then unwound: USD/JPY fell 14% in two days to 8 Oct [Y].

Price [Y]

DateS&P 500
Closing peak17 Jul 19981186.75
Closing low31 Aug 1998957.28 (−6.8% that day)
Rescue-day bounce23 Sep1066.09
Intraday low8 Oct 1998923.32 (close 959.44)
New closing high23 Nov 19981188.21
  • Depth −19.3% closing (to 31 Aug), −22.4% intraday (to 8 Oct). The brief's "final low 8 Oct" is right only intraday; the closing low was 31 Aug.
  • Small caps peaked in April: Russell 2000 −36.9% (21 Apr → 8 Oct) and still below its April high a year later.
  • Nasdaq −29.5% (20 Jul → 8 Oct).

Fed [FA; federalreserve.gov/boarddocs/press/general/1998/19981015/]

  • 29 Sep: −25bp. The market sold off: −3.1% and −3.0% on the next two days.
  • 15 Oct: intermeeting −25bp. S&P +4.2% that day.
  • 17 Nov: −25bp. Hikes resumed 30 Jun 1999.

Earnings. EPS −5% in 1998, then +28% in 1999 [EPS]. No recession.

Credit. Peaked a week after the equity low and stayed wide: TED max 1.66 and Baa–10y max 2.77, both on 16 Oct [TED, BAA].

Order of signs at the low

  1. Capitulation 31 Aug: −6.8%, VIX 44.28, SPY volume 2.5 times its 50-day average (computed here).
  2. VIX stayed above 40 through September.
  3. LTCM rescue 23 Sep; first cut 29 Sep disappointed.
  4. 8 Oct: yen spike, intraday undercut of 3.5%, close back above the 31 Aug close, VIX closing peak 45.74.
  5. The Russell 2000 and Nasdaq closed at clear new lows that day; the S&P closing low held. Large caps diverged from small caps.
  6. Surprise cut on 15 Oct started the V.
  7. Credit spreads peaked 16 Oct.
  8. New high 23 Nov.

No breadth-thrust signal [BAM].

What led afterwards. Large-cap technology and internet: Nasdaq +54.5% from 8 Oct to year-end. From the 8 Oct close to 31 Dec: Amazon +273%, Cisco +99%, Microsoft +52% [Y]. Small caps lagged badly.

29 SepFed −25bp

15 OctIntermeeting cut
17 NovThird cut
23 NovNew closing high

---

A5. 2011: debt ceiling, downgrade and the euro crisis

Cause. Two overlapping shocks.

  • US: debt-ceiling deal signed 2 Aug; S&P cut the US to AA+ on Friday evening 5 Aug [https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_federal_government_credit-rating_downgrades].
  • Europe: contagion fears for Italy and Spain; short-selling bans on 11 Aug [https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/August_2011_stock_markets_fall].

Price [Y]

DateS&P 500
Closing peak29 Apr 20111363.61
Pre-slide level22 Jul1345.02
First low8 Aug1119.46 (−6.66%)
Closing low3 Oct 20111099.23
Intraday low4 Oct 20111074.77 (close 1123.95)
  • Depth −19.4% closing, −21.6% intraday. Most of the damage (−16.8%) came in 11 sessions from 22 Jul to 8 Aug.
  • Then an eight-week base between roughly 1120 and 1220.
  • First close above the old high: 24 Feb 2012. Russell 2000 −29.6%.

Fed. Already at zero, so it used guidance and the balance sheet.

  • 9 Aug: rates low "at least through mid-2013" [https://www.federalreserve.gov/newsevents/pressreleases/monetary20110809a.htm]. S&P +4.7%.
  • 21 Sep: Operation Twist, $400bn [https://www.federalreserve.gov/newsevents/pressreleases/monetary20110921a.htm]. S&P −2.9% then −3.2%.
  • 30 Nov: six central banks cut dollar swap pricing [https://www.federalreserve.gov/newsevents/pressreleases/monetary20111130a.htm]. S&P +4.3%.

ECB. Draghi took office 1 Nov. Two 36-month LTROs announced 8 Dec; first allotted 21 Dec [https://www.ecb.europa.eu/press/pr/date/2011/html/pr111208_1.en.html]. "Whatever it takes" came later, 26 Jul 2012.

Earnings. EPS rose 12% in 2011 [EPS]. No US recession.

Credit. Baa–10y max 3.39 on 27 Sep, a week before the equity low [BAA]. Bank funding stress (TED) kept rising to January 2012 [TED].

Order of signs at the low

  1. VIX closing peak 48.00 on 8 Aug, the first low. SPY volume 3.1 times average on 8 Aug and 3.2 times on 9 Aug (computed here).
  2. Fed pledge and a 4.7% reversal on 9 Aug.
  3. Retest on 19–22 Aug held.
  4. 3–4 Oct: undercut by 1.8% closing and 2.4% intraday, with a lower VIX (45.45) and ordinary volume (SPY 1.1 times average on 3 Oct), then a reversal on 4 Oct.
  5. +3.4% on 10 Oct; follow-through day on 18 Oct [QE]; +3.4% on 27 Oct.
  6. Higher low 25 Nov (1158.67).
  7. Swap lines 30 Nov; LTRO in December.

No breadth-thrust signal in 2011–12 [BAM]. Quantifiable Edges noted this was the first rally since 1971 to reach a 50-day high from an 8% drawdown without a follow-through day on its three indices until 18 Oct [QE].

What led afterwards (3 Oct 2011 – 30 Mar 2012) [Y]: homebuilders (XHB) +70%, Apple +60%, financials +40%, discretionary +33%, technology +31%, SPY +28%, utilities +7%.

Timeline

DateEvent
29 Apr 2011S&P closing peak 1363.61
2 AugDebt-ceiling law signed
5 AugS&P downgrades the US
8 Aug−6.7%; VIX 48.00
9 AugFed "mid-2013" pledge

18 OctFollow-through day
30 NovDollar swap pricing cut
8 and 21 DecLTRO announced; first allotment
24 Feb 2012New closing high

A6. 2015–16: China, oil and the first hike

Cause. Slowing China and the yuan devaluation, collapsing oil, the end of QE and an approaching first hike, against an earnings recession [https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015%E2%80%932016_stock_market_selloff].

Price [Y, calc]

PointDateLevel
Record close21 May 20152130.82
First-leg closing low25 Aug 20151867.61 (−12.35%)
First-leg intraday low24 Aug 20151867.01
Retest28–29 Sep 2015close 1881.77; intraday 1871.91 (held)
Recovery high3 Nov 20152109.79 (+12.97% in 49 trading days)
January low20 Jan 2016intraday 1812.29, close 1859.33
Final low11 Feb 2016close 1829.08, intraday 1810.10
  • Total depth −14.16% closing, −15.21% intraday, over 183 trading days. It never reached −20%. The second leg (3 Nov → 11 Feb) was −13.31%.

The 24 Aug open [SEC staff note, sec.gov/marketstructure/research/equity_market_volatility.pd SPY opened −5.2% and hit −7.8% by 9:35. There were 1,278 limit-up/limit-down halts, 1,058 of them in 327 exchange-traded products. VIX hit 53.29 intraday and closed at 40.74 [Y].

Fed.

  • Hiked 25bp on 16 Dec 2015 [https://www.federalreserve.gov/newsevents/pressreleases/monetary20151216a.htm].
  • On 16 Mar 2016 the median end-2016 projection fell from 1.4% to 0.9%, four hikes cut to two [https://www.federalreserve.gov/monetarypolicy/fomcprojtabl20160316.htm]. The next hike was Dec 2016.
  • G20 in Shanghai 26–27 Feb 2016 pledged no competitive devaluations [https://g20.utoronto.ca/2016/160227-finance-en.html]. The "Shanghai Accord" is an analyst label.

Earnings. Q1 2016 was the fourth straight year-on-year decline (−6.7% blended, FactSet via cbsnews.com/amp/news/the-earnings-recession-is-a-heavy-weigh).

Credit. Baa–10y rose from 2.75% to a peak of 3.63% on 11 Feb 2016, the day of the equity low [BAA]. HYG's closing low was the same day [Y]. WTI settled at $26.21 that day [https://www.ogj.com/general-interest/article/17251588/market-watch-nymex-march-crude-oil-price-settles-near-26bbl]. ICE high-yield and energy-HY peak levels unverified.

Order of signs at the low

  1. VIX peaked in Aug 2015 (40.74 close). At the Feb low it closed at only 28.14 [Y].
  2. New lows peaked on 20 Jan: about 1,350–1,400 NYSE new lows, over 42% of issues [https://sentimentrader.com/blog/breadth-flush-pt-2-new-lows].
  3. The 11 Feb price low came with fewer new lows, a positive divergence [https://thereformedbroker.com/2016/02/12/a-positive-divergence-at-last/]. Oil and credit bottomed the same day.
  4. 17 Feb: follow-through day, day 4, Nasdaq +2.21%.
  5. 25 Feb: close above the 50-day (calc).
  6. 3 Mar: breadth thrust on the S&P 1500, led by small and mid caps, unlike the failed Oct 2015 thrust [https://articles.stockcharts.com/article/articles-arthurhill-2016-03-market-environment-turns---october-vs-february---breadth-thrust-triggers/].
  7. 11 Mar: close above the 200-day; Deemer 20-day breakaway momentum the same day [BAM].
  8. 16 Mar: Fed dots cut.
  9. 23 Mar: over 90% of S&P stocks above their 50-day [https://thereformedbroker.com/2016/03/23/chart-o-the-day-as-extended-as-it-ever-gets/].
  10. 25 Apr: golden cross. 11 Jul 2016: new record close.

Retest. Twice. Sep 2015 held above August. Feb 2016 undercut the 20 Jan intraday low by about 2 points and reversed.

What led afterwards (total return from the 11 Feb 2016 close) [Y, calc]

The most beaten-down groups led first. Banks and semis were second-half leaders; staples and utilities lagged.

A7. 2020: COVID crash

Cause. Pandemic shutdown, plus an oil-price war from 9 Mar [https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_stock_market_crash].

Price [Y, F, calc]

PointDateLevel
Record close19 Feb 20203386.15
Low23 Mar 2020close 2237.40, intraday 2191.86
  • Depth −33.92% closing, −35.41% intraday, in 23 trading days.
  • Big days: 9 Mar −7.60%, 12 Mar −9.51%, 13 Mar +9.29%, 16 Mar −11.98%.
  • Level-1 circuit breakers on 9, 12, 16 and 18 Mar.

Fed (press releases at federalreserve.gov/newsevents/pressreleases/)

  • 3 Mar: −50bp [monetary20200303a.htm].
  • 15 Mar (Sunday): −100bp to zero, plus at least $700bn of purchases [monetary20200315a.htm].
  • 17–18 Mar: commercial-paper, dealer and money-fund facilities [monetary20200317a.htm, monetary20200318a.htm].
  • 23 Mar, 8:00am, the morning of the low: purchases "in the amounts needed", plus corporate-credit facilities [monetary20200323b.htm].
  • CARES Act ($2.2tn) signed 27 Mar [https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CARES_Act].

Earnings. CY2020 EPS −11.4% [FactSet, go.factset.com/hubfs/Website/Resources%20Section/Research%20

Credit. ICE high-yield OAS peaked at 1,087bp on 23 Mar 2020, the same day as the equity low [https://rpc.cfainstitute.org/blogs/enterprising-investor/2025/think-weve-seen-the-last-1000-bps-high-yield-spread-think-again]. Spreads eased after the corporate-facility announcement [https://www.federalreserve.gov/econres/notes/feds-notes/the-corporate-bond-market-crises-and-the-government-response-20201007.htm].

Order of signs

  1. VIX closing record 82.69 on 16 Mar; intraday peak 85.47 on 18 Mar. At the 23 Mar price low it was 61.59, so volatility peaked a week before price [Y, F].
  2. SPY volume peaked on 12 Mar (2.6 times its 50-day average), seven sessions before the low (computed here).
  3. Policy arrived at 8am on the low day; high-yield spreads peaked that day.
  4. 24–26 Mar: +9.38%, +1.15%, +6.24%, a +17.55% three-day run. Each day had over 80% up issues and up volume [https://sentimentrader.com/blog/a-historic-3-day-buying-thrust-is-changing-sentiment/].
  5. 2 Apr: follow-through day on the S&P (+2.28%); Nasdaq on 6 Apr (computed here; secondary blogs agree).
  6. Zweig thrust: sources disagree. SentimenTrader in 2023 says none triggered because the move "took a few days too long" [https://sentimentrader.com/blog/the-holy-grail-of-breadth-thrusts-has-triggered]. Deemer's breakaway momentum fired on 3 Jun 2020 [BAM].
  7. Close above the 50-day on 17 Apr and the 200-day on 27 May, by which time the index was 35.7% off the low (computed here).
  8. Record close 18 Aug 2020.

Retest. None. The only pullback low was 2470.50 on 1 Apr, 10.4% above the low.

The first bounce was the most-crushed groups (energy, banks, homebuilders). Durable leadership was growth, semis, homebuilders and later small caps. Staples, utilities and health care lagged.

A8. 2022: hiking-cycle bear

Cause. Inflation at 40-year highs, then the fastest hiking cycle since the 1980s, with balance-sheet runoff from June [https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_stock_market_decline].

Price [Y, F, calc]

PointDateLevel
Nasdaq Composite peak19 Nov 202116,057.44
S&P record close3 Jan 20224796.56
June low16 Jun 2022close 3666.77 (−23.55%)
Summer high16 Aug 20224305.20 (+17.41% in 41 trading days)
Closing low12 Oct 20223577.03 (−25.43%, 195 trading days)
Intraday low13 Oct 20223491.58 (−27.54%)
Nasdaq Composite closing low28 Dec 202210,213.29 (−36.4%)
  • The summer rally's intraday high on 16 Aug was 4325.28 against a 200-day average of 4326.18. It failed within one point.
  • 13 Oct was CPI day: an outside reversal, closing +2.60%.
  • The Nasdaq made its closing low on 28 Dec, but its intraday low was 13 Oct. The Russell 2000's closing low was 16 Jun and it held in October.

Fed [FedT]

  • 16 Mar +25bp, 4 May +50bp, then +75bp on 15 Jun, 27 Jul, 21 Sep and 2 Nov; 14 Dec +50bp; then +25bp four times to 26 Jul 2023.
  • Powell at Jackson Hole, 26 Aug: "pain to households and businesses" [https://www.federalreserve.gov/newsevents/speech/powell20220826a.htm]. S&P −3.4%.
  • 10 Nov: October CPI 7.7% [https://www.bls.gov/news.release/archives/cpi_11102022.htm]. S&P +5.54%.
  • SVB: Bank Term Funding Program on 12 Mar 2023; S&P closing low 3855.76 on 13 Mar 2023.

Earnings. Year-on-year declines in Q4 2022, Q1 and Q2 2023; Q3 2023 was the first growth [FactSet, go.factset.com/hubfs/Website/Resources%20Section/Research%20

Credit. Baa–10y peaked at 2.42% in July 2022 and was lower (2.27%) at the October equity low. Credit did not confirm October [BAA]. ICE high-yield peak (about 6%, July 2022) unverified.

No capitulation. VIX's highest 2022 close was 36.45 on 7 Mar. At the lows it closed at 34.02 (13 Jun) and 33.57 (12 Oct) [Y]. VIX never closed more than 2% above three-month VIX during the decline (computed here). The heaviest SPY volume near the October low was on 30 Sep, not at the low (computed here).

After the rally that held: −7.3% to 28 Dec, +10.5% to 2 Feb 2023, −7.8% to 13 Mar 2023.

Follow-through days. By my coded test, the S&P had three in 2022 before the low (9 Feb, 16 Mar, 24 Jun), all failed; the Nasdaq had four (8 Feb, 18 Mar, 17 May, 23 Jun), three failed outright and the June one rallied 2× before the index made a new low (computed here; definition in B2). A blog counted 26 May as IBD's "fourth this year" [https://stageanalysis.net/blog/294655/us-stocks-watchlist-26-may-2022]. IBD's official count is unverified. The one that worked was 21 Oct 2022 (S&P +2.37%).

Breadth thrusts.

  • 4 Jan 2022: S&P 1500 thrust, a failure SentimenTrader itself flags [https://sentimentrader.com/blog/here-come-the-breadth-thrust-buy-signals].
  • 28 Jul 2022: S&P 1500 thrust; price made new lows in October but was higher a year later [same].
  • 12 Jan 2023: NYSE breakaway momentum, 2.16 [BAM], with Nasdaq, small-cap, discretionary and high-yield thrusts alongside [https://sentimentrader.com/blog/a-cluster-of-breadth-thrust-buy-signals-bodes-well-for-stocks].
  • 31 Mar 2023: Zweig Breadth Thrust [https://sentimentrader.com/blog/the-holy-grail-of-breadth-thrusts-has-triggered]. A second in Nov 2023 (exact day unverified).

A9. April 2025: tariff crash

Cause. "Liberation Day" reciprocal tariffs announced after the close on 2 Apr 2025 [https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_stock_market_crash].

Price [Y, F, calc]

PointDateLevel
Record close19 Feb 20256144.15
First close below 200-day10 Mar 2025—
10% correction13 Mar 20255521.52 (−10.13%)
Two-day drop3–4 Apr−4.84% and −5.97% (−10.53% combined)
Intraday low7 Apr 20254835.04 (−21.35% from the intraday high)
Closing low8 Apr 20254982.77 (−18.90%, 34 trading days)
  • It reached −20% intraday but never on a close.
  • 7 Apr: a false "90-day pause" headline produced a 4835–5246 range.
  • 8 Apr: gapped up to 5267.47, then sold off to close −1.57%.
  • 9 Apr: pause announced. S&P +9.52%; Nasdaq +12.16%, the biggest since 2008. 10 Apr: −3.46%.
  • Nasdaq Composite −24.3%; Russell 2000 −27.9%.

Fed. On hold at 4.25–4.50%; no cut until Sep 2025 [FedT].

  • Powell, 4 Apr: tariffs "significantly larger than expected"; "well positioned to wait" [https://www.federalreserve.gov/newsevents/speech/powell20250404a.htm].
  • Powell, 16 Apr: goals may be "in tension" [https://www.federalreserve.gov/newsevents/speech/powell20250416a.htm]. S&P −2.24%.
  • There was no Fed rescue. The policy trigger for the low was the White House.

Treasuries and dollar [Y]. 10-year yield 3.98% (4 Apr) → 4.49% (11 Apr). Dollar index 103.81 (2 Apr) → 98.28 (21 Apr). Stocks, bonds and the dollar fell together. A basis-trade unwind as the mechanism is unverified.

Earnings. No earnings recession. Q1 2025 EPS +13.3%; Q2 estimates were cut 4.0% between 31 Mar and 29 May [FactSet, go.factset.com/hubfs/Website/Resources%20Section/Research%20

Credit. High-yield OAS rose from 2.68% (19 Feb) to a peak of 4.61% on 7 Apr, and was 3.15% by 12 May [F-HY]. Mild next to 2016 or 2020.

Order of signs

  1. 7 Apr: VIX intraday peak 60.13 at the open, high-yield spread peak, intraday price low, and the heaviest SPY volume (4.1 times its 50-day average, computed here).
  2. 8 Apr: VIX closing peak 52.33 and the closing price low. Volatility and price peaked together, unlike 2020.
  3. 9 Apr: policy reversal, a +9.5% day.
  4. 21 Apr: higher low at 5158.20 with VIX at 33.82, 3.5% above the closing low.
  5. 22–24 Apr: +2.51%, +1.67%, +2.03%. Follow-through day 22 Apr (computed here; day 9, +2.51%).
  6. 24 Apr: Zweig Breadth Thrust, the first since Nov 2023 [https://www.investing.com/analysis/sp-500-breadth-thrust-signals-a-new-bullish-phase-as-death-cross-fears-fade-200660022; fool.com/investing/2025/04/26/the-sp-500-just-did-something- Counts of prior signals vary by source (16 to 20).
  7. Nine straight up days to 2 May. Close above the 50-day on 1 May.
  8. 12 May: Geneva truce [https://www.whitehouse.gov/briefings-statements/2025/05/joint-statement-on-u-s-china-economic-and-trade-meeting-in-geneva/]. S&P +3.26% and back above the 200-day.
  9. Record close 27 Jun 2025, 55 trading days after the low.

Moving-average crosses were late both ways: death cross on 14 Apr, after the low; golden cross on 1 Jul, after the new high.

A10. Corrections during Fed hiking cycles

Closing basis [Y, calc]; hike dates from [FedT]. "What ended it" is a reading of the dates unless a source is given.

EpisodePeak → trough (close)DepthWhat ended it
198725 Aug → 4 Dec 1987−33.5%Fed's 20 Oct liquidity statement and easing.
19942 Feb → 4 Apr 1994−8.9%The low came after the second hike. The index then ranged through five more hikes and made a new high two weeks after the last one.
1997 (single hike, 25 Mar)18 Feb → 11 Apr 1997−9.6%No further hikes; new high 5 May 1997.
1999 (hikes 30 Jun, 24 Aug, 16 Nov)16 Jul → 15 Oct 1999−12.1%No policy change. New high on 16 Nov 1999, the day of the third hike.
2000, Jan–Feb (hike 2 Feb)31 Dec 1999 → 25 Feb 2000−9.3%New high 21 Mar 2000, also a hike day.
2000 top24 Mar → 14 Apr 2000−11.2%, then −49.1% to 9 Oct 2002Nothing ended it. The last hike (16 May 2000) came after the top; cuts from Jan 2001 did not stop the bear.
2004 (first hike 30 Jun)11 Feb → 12 Aug 2004−8.2% over 126 trading daysThe low came two days after the second hike. Hikes continued at 25bp a meeting; new high 4 Nov 2004.
20057 Mar → 20 Apr 2005−7.2%Hikes continued; new high 14 Jul 2005.
20065 May → 13 Jun 2006−7.7% in 26 trading daysThe low came two weeks before the 17th and last hike (29 Jun). Pause on 8 Aug 2006 [https://www.federalreserve.gov/newsevents/pressreleases/monetary20060808a.htm].
2015–16 (one hike, 16 Dec 2015)21 May 2015 → 11 Feb 2016−14.2%Oil and credit low on 11 Feb; G20; dots cut from four hikes to two on 16 Mar 2016.
2018, Feb26 Jan → 8 Feb 2018−10.2% in 9 trading daysA volatility unwind, not a policy change. Retest held on 2 Apr 2018; hikes continued.
2018, Q420 Sep → 24 Dec 2018−19.8%Last hike 19 Dec 2018. "Patient" FOMC statement on 30 Jan 2019 [https://www.federalreserve.gov/newsevents/pressreleases/monetary20190130a.htm].
20223 Jan → 12 Oct 2022−25.4%Inflation rolling over (10 Nov CPI) and smaller hikes. The last hike was Jul 2023, nine months after the price low.

Pattern in these rows

  • In 1994, 2004 and 2006 the correction was 8–9% and bottomed while hikes were still under way. Earnings were rising in each.
  • Drops of 19% or more (1987, Q4 2018, 2022) needed a Fed change of stance or pace.
  • In 2000 the end of hiking did not help.
  • The present decline is also inside a hiking phase. The Fed's table shows a 25bp increase effective 17 Sep 2026, to 3.75–4.00% [FedT], and the local effective-funds series steps from 3.63% to 3.88% that day (fred_DFF.csv). The S&P's record close was 7818.93 on 6 Oct 2026; the 20 Nov close of 6910.39 is −11.6% (local data). The other agent's now-2026.md covers this.
